#2927. Packers should lean on running back Kaleb Johnson after their bye

The bright spots of the Packers' Week 10 loss to the Vikings are few and far between, but running back Kaleb Johnson was surely among them.

"I thought he did a nice job," LaFleur said of Johnson's 10-carry, 42-yard outing. "I liked how he was hitting the hole, running with good pad level, pressing the line of scrimmage. I thought that was a positive performance."

If there's a big takeaway to be had here, it's that Johnson should be getting the lion's share of carries going forward, and probably deserves more snaps, too.

Josh Jacobs remains a mystery. He's on track to return in relatively short order from finger surgery, but a potential suspension still looms like the sword of Damocles. He wasn't overly effective as a runner in his brief return to the lineup, either. Across three games, Jacobs carried 36 times for just 80 yards, an abysmal 2.2 yards per carry. Even the perpetually disappointing MarShawn Lloyd has managed 2.7 yards per attempt this year.

Speaking of Lloyd, even if he's better than Jacobs this year, he's obviously not the future or the present at the position. Kudos to him for making it all the way back from his various injury issues over the last couple of seasons, but it's abundantly clear he can't get the job done. It's hard to foresee him improving much even if the Packers make major strides on the offensive line.

Chris Brooks, meanwhile, is a solid but limited player. It's probably better to think of him as a fullback at this point, a latter day John Kuhn who mostly finds his way to the field because he's not going to screw it up for the Packers in pass protection.

And since Pierre Strong Jr. has now been released, that leaves us with Johnson as the last remaining back on the roster, and perhaps the most viable.

The big problem with Johnson to this point has been his limited value in the passing game. You can see what the Packers think of him there by just look at his snap usage. He was on the field for 13 snaps against the Vikings and carried the ball on 10 of them. As a rule, they don't want him catching passes or blocking for Jordan Love.

But LaFleur did call out Johnson's pass blocking as his most notable contribution on Sunday. Johnson stonewalled a blitzing linebacker on a 19-yard completion.

"The thing I was most pleased with was one of the protections when we hit [Tucker Kraft], he came across and picked up Eric Wilson on the blitz right in the B gap," LaFleur said.

You don't have to be a tape grinder to see what LaFleur is talking about. Johnson lines up to Jordan Love's left on the play, but alertly comes across the formation to pick off Wilson just as he jets past Travis Glover. Love completes the pass to Kraft, and the Packers would go on to score three points on the drive, cutting the Vikings' lead to just 10 points. In theory, it could have been a pivotal play.

The fact is that Johnson is the only back doing anything even close to making pivotal plays this season. The Packers have only had a back rush for 40 or more yards in a game three times this season, and Johnson is responsible for all three of those occurrences. The Packers will probably never have a good running game this season, but if they're going to do anything at all on the ground, it's probably going to be because Johnson is getting the work done.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2926. Pistons vs. Raptors final score: Cade vs. Scottie battle goes to Raps

Two former teammates and two Eastern Conference teams traded body blows all night. A Cade Cunningham 3-pointer to put the Pistons up 102-101 with 20 seconds left was answered by a Scottie Barnes driving layup over two defenders to give the Raptors the lead they would not relinquish. Again, I'm going to point to the big black hole in the middle of Detroit's lineup. A combination of John Collins and Paul Reed as your only big men is just not going to get it done in the NBA. The Pistons need Jalen Duren to come back desperately.

#2925. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2924. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2923. Cowboys’ Brian Schottenheimer Accused of ‘Saving Face’ With Coach Move

It was not a banner week for the Dallas Cowboys, who came off as something of an amateurish circus act with the way the coaching staff was juggled. Coach Brian Schottenheimer removed play-calling duties from Christian Parker, the first-year defensive coordinator who was Schottenheimer's personal choice for the job, leaving Parker in the awkward position of being a coordinator with little to coordinate.

Parker said the decision was a "significant" disappointment to him. He considered resigning.

In a pained press conference, he said about quitting, "Initially in your mind, you're thinking about all the things, and in my opinion, that would've been running from it. At the end of the day, I've got a job to do, and my job is to be the defensive coordinator of the Dallas Cowboys."

There's no question that Parker did not do a particularly good job in the 10 games he spent calling plays for the Cowboys. The team is 4-6 and the defense ranks No. 30 in points allowed and 32nd in yardage allowed. The tackling has been miserable, and the team was exposed badly by the 49ers, who had 584 yards of offense, in Week 10.

But according to Jonathan Jones of The Athletic, the feeling around the NFL is that this move was done so that Schottenheimer could take some of the heat off himself for what has been a disappointing year most expected to finish with a deep playoff run, and possibly even a Super Bowl.

Jones quoted a source: "This was a move for Schotty to save face,"

He added another source: "It became an easy out on the season, and they took it. He was a Schotty hire-and-fire."

Owner Jerry Jones has made it clear he would not fire Schottenheimer during the season. But now that he has thrown one coordinator under the bus, the pressure is on Schottenheimer to show results. The Cowboys play the 1-8 Titans at 1 p.m. ET on Sunday at AT&T Stadium.

#2920. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2917. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2916. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#2914. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2913. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

We've got to give Rovella playing time so that he can regain fitness, then he can give us an important hand.'

While some coaches demand perfection in ball retention, Gattuso is willing to accept a certain level of technical error in exchange for the suffocating pressure his side applies to opponents. Lazio will now turn their attention to their next Serie A fixture, an away clash against Torino on November 28, before returning to the Stadio Olimpico to host Atalanta on December 6.

'I don't mind losing the ball with misplaced passes that much, because when you play at a high tempo, that can happen. I am more interested in the mentality of the side and how they are hanging in there under pressure. I am satisfied, you always want something more, but at this tempo there are going to be some misplaced passes. We can improve our reading of the game, as for example today when we struggled to get through the middle, we needed to spread the game out wide.'

#2910. Bayern chief addresses Musiala pressure amid growing concerns and spotlight

Hainer stressed that Musiala's health comes before any sporting or financial considerations, insisting that the club is doing everything possible to help him return to his previous level.

"Our foremost wish is to see him back on the pitch," Hainer explained, adding that sporting directors Christoph Freund and Max Eberl remain in constant contact with the player's representatives.

Hainer subsequently clarified that he did not believe Bayern were aware of Musiala's neurological condition when he signed his contract extension. He also insisted that there was no intention to put pressure on the player.

Musiala's medication reportedly remains a significant challenge, with side effects complicating his recovery and making a consistent return to full fitness difficult.

For now, Bayern's message is clear: Musiala will receive all the support and time necessary to make his comeback.

#2909. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2908. Steelers KR Kaden Wetjen could be benched for undrafted free agent

The Pittsburgh Steelers selected wide receiver Kaden Wetjen in the fourth round of the 2026 NFL Draft out of Iowa. Listing Wetjen as a wide receiver was a bit of a stretch, though, as he was really known for being one of the nation's most electrifying return specialists during his time with the Hawkeyes.

To this point in his NFL career, Pittsburgh has yet to reap the benefits of selecting the return man in the fourth round. This could explain why one Steelers insider believes that Wetjen could be upended by an undrafted free agent as soon as this weekend.

Appearing on 93.7 The Fan on Friday, Ray Fittipaldo of The Pittsburgh Post-Gazette suggested that the Steelers could replace Wetjen in favor of the highly recommended Max Hurleman.

Fittiplado reported earlier in the week that Rodgers had mentioned that he would like to see Hurleman to get some reps on special teams, a sound explanation for his belief that the undrafted free agent could get a helmet this Sunday.

Many members of the Steelers organization have sung the praises of Hurleman in the past week, including Aaron Rodgers, Mike McCarthy, and DK Metcalf. Earlier in the week, the team opened up Hurleman's 21-day practice window, clearing the path for him to return from injured reserve.

The receiver had put together two strong training camps with the team but has yet to appear in a game. There seems to be too much smoke to ignore right now. Pittsburgh has plans for Hurleman, and he could get a helmet as early as this week.

#2907. Jordan Stout tells The Post how he overcame ‘mental hump’ to become Giants’ game-changing ‘weapon’

Giants punter Jordan Stout takes a timeout to kick around some Q&A with Post columnist Steve Serby.

A: It's such a unique skill. … There's a sweet spot on the ball about the size of a quarter. Tell me another position like this. It's like I'm doing a max power clean with a moving ball while I'm moving. And every time, you gotta make contact in the perfect spot or you shank it. I went a year-and-a-half without a shank, and I feel like the attention to detail, the focus you have to have every single day, you can't lose it for a moment or something bad'll happen. I love how hard it is.

A: The one thing I don't like about punting is, at times I feel like I can't do anything, I can't help the team. I do feel like a weapon this year, I feel like we're playing close games. If I play the ball that I'm capable of, then we win, and if I don't, we lose. And I like the responsibility. I like being put in the situations that are the most stressful because that means it's important.

Giants punter Jordan Stout (15) punts during the fourth quarter against the Commanders at MetLife Stadium in East Rutherford, N.J., Nov. 12, 2026. Charles Wenzelberg/NY Post

Q: How many punts do you have in your tool bag?

A: I can get real technical about it, but in reality I have about five or six punts. If I got real technical I could say a right high ball is one, a right low ball is two, a left high ball is one, a left low ball is two, boomerang right, boomerang left, boomerang middle. But in reality, there's like five to six punts that I actually have that are different.

Q: Any one in particular that's your favorite?

A: I like 'em all the same. No discrimination (smile).

Q: You're on record saying that you want to change the game?

A: That's my goal. I want to be the best of all time, and I want to leave a legacy and change the game, and I feel like changing the game goes hand in hand with being the best of all time. My goal in the offseason is to create punts that no one's ever done before. It's hard because people have been doing this for a long time. But from year to year, last year I started hitting a punt that no one had hit, and then this year I'm hitting a punt that, to my knowledge, no one has hit.

A: It's called a boomerang, a banana, whatever.

Q: You did the boomerang last year, right?

A: I did, yeah, but the one this year's called a ribbon. It's a play on the boomerang, but if you think of it from my perspective, the way the ball flies in the air is like a ribbon. So if it goes up left, back right like a boomerang but then it comes back left. And it's just a lot of repetition. It started as a mistake. The sweet spot on the ball is like an inch, or a centimeter sometimes. I didn't get it right until after preseason. We hit it for the first time three or four games ago, and I've hit it once since then. No one's touched it yet.

A: It's somewhat selfish in a way, but I feel like I have to have a selfish mindset to I want to be the best, but I want to punt. I want to punt every drive. It's selfish, but I feel like if I don't have that mentality that anytime I'm called on, maybe I won't be ready. I don't care, I'll say it, like that's kind of what happened last game when I had that short [12-yard] punt. Right before that, three minutes left, I had a punt in the back of my end zone. In my head, that was gonna be my last punt. I wanted to show (laugh) the world, the coaches, the team, like, this is what they brought me here for, and I delivered [68 yards]. And then after that, I went to the sideline, took a deep breath, like, "OK, I'm done." Next thing I know, I'm up again. I thought we were gonna hit a field goal, last second — punt. So it's just a lesson, and it's a learning opportunity that until the clock hits zero, you can't let up.

Q: What was it like for you watching the film of that punt?

A: It pissed me off. But I'm not worried in the slightest. I'm at a point in my career now where I know what I'm capable of. If that woulda happened two years ago, I would still be worried about it and stressed about it, but yeah, it's gone. I'm glad it happened, it's fuel to the fire.

A: I want them to be afraid when they're playing me because they don't know what's coming next. A lot of time punters, they line up like they're aimed right, they go right. They line up on the right side and they aim left, they go left. My game, no one knows where it's going besides the guys on our team, and I feel like that gives us an advantage. Also, the more of these punts we have, the longer the other teams focus on it in meetings, and the more they think about it, and the more they think about it, they psych themselves out, like, "Oh, if he hits this punt, let's just leave it be because it's tough." In my head, that's what they're doing because when we're going up against a punter that has a bag of tricks, that's kind of the feeling I get — maybe not specifically this team, but in past years, that's what I've seen.

Q: Speaking of which, when did you see a sports psychologist?

A: Fully transparent, in my third year, the last five games of the year when I was at my worst because I wanted to know how to get over that mental hump.

A: At the time, the mental hump came from bad weather. It was raining, it was windy and it was cold, and if it wasn't raining, it was snowing. It was the toughest five-game stretch I've ever had. I would do everything right in practice and then I'd get in the games. By the way, Baltimore was a very, very hard place to play. It didn't seem to matter what I did, 'cause I was doing everything right. On-field performance was bad. What did I learn from that experience? You gotta control what you can control. If I make good contact on the ball and it's still not good, there's nothing I can do about it. I can't work myself up about it, it is what it is.

New York Giants place kicker Dominic Zvada (34) celebrates with punter Jordan Stout (15) after kicking a field goal on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026, in Philadelphia. Charles Wenzelberg/NY Post

A: It sucked! Yeah, it was awful. Like I said, I want to be the best of all time, and when I'm not the best of all time but like lower half of the league in those last five games, it's brutal because I know I put in the work.

Q: What was your wife Julia's level of support?

A: The whole time she's just saying, "Don't worry about it." And she actually pushed me to see this sports psychologist because she didn't have the answers and she wanted to point me in the right direction of someone who did have the answers. But she did everything right, she said, "You're fine, nothing bad's gonna happen, you got it … see a sports psychologist (laugh)."

Q: Was it Julia who said once, "Punting is hot?"

A: I don't know, I guess punting is hot, I don't know (laugh).

A: She is very caring. She isn't afraid to be herself. She gives it to you straight, she's a great friend because of that, she's never gonna beat around the bush. In any moment I've ever struggled, she's always been my backbone.

Q: What makes coach John Harbaugh a great leader?

A: I feel like he is extremely consistent. Any time he gets up and he gives a message, he means it, and it's from his heart. He's not just saying what he thinks we want to hear, he's saying what he believes in.

A: My rookie year I was struggling with pooch punts in the plus-50 area. So that week prior, a lot of people were sick, and the medical staff asked me to wear a mask because I was exposed to the virus even though I wasn't sick. So he came up to me: "Hey, uh, you sick?" I'm like "No." He's like, "Oh, what's the half life on the sickness you have?" Like, "Oh, I'm not sick." He's like, "Oh, and by the way, when are we gonna get those pooches fixed?" It threw me off a little bit. At the same time, I love that about him. I was still doing really well, but he saw one thing I was doing wrong and he let me know that he saw it. And then the next day he comes up to me and he says, "Hey, you know I love you, right?" He didn't apologize, but he's like, "You're good, right?" I'm like, "Yeah, of course I'm good." And I told him I like it when a coach does that.

Q: The irony of playing against Jaguars punter Logan Cook on Sunday.

A: Yeah, what a baller, in the boomerang.

A: Yeah, absolutely. I tweaked his a little bit, his boomerang, but he's the guy who I feel like has had the most success, at least in my time in the league, that I've been able to see him, and be like, "Wow, that is effective." When he first started doing the boomerang, every time we played him, or anyone else played him, they didn't catch the ball because they were worried that they wouldn't be able to. The thing that he did well is he made the ball move a lot in the air. The thing that I didn't think he did well with it was it didn't go far enough. So what I did is, I tweaked the position of the ball a little bit because I wanted to hit it but I wanted to hit farther. I feel like that would ruin my stats (laugh) if it didn't go far enough. But without him, Johnny Hekker, Sam Koch, those guys like doing it in the first place, who even knows if I would have? And that's what I mean when I say I want to change the game. I want to expand the game, I want to make everybody better because of how good I am.

A: I hope it rains. … 'Cause then the returner is gonna have a harder time. It's about mentality, right? If I go into the game knowing that I'm gonna have a good game, then I have an edge. Also, on top of that, if it's windy, when it rains the ball gets heavier, and it cuts through the wind better.

Q: Describe Giants fullback Patrick Ricard, who was also a teammate with the Ravens.

A: Great guy, super good friend. He's a nerd at heart, but you wouldn't believe it on the field 'cause he's badass.

A: He loves video games, he loves Pokémon cards, he's a collector. But the thing I love so much about Pat is he's not afraid to be himself.

A: Baller. I've never seen someone with better body control. He's a great leader, he's still learning to speak up, he's a quiet guy, but when he does speak, he has really good things to say.

Q: What do you like best about this team?

A: I love that everyone has something to prove. There's no egos. All there is is just guys with something to prove, and because of that they're excited to come in every day and work hard. Harbs is tough. Harbs has tough practices. And it works here because everyone's bought in. What he says goes, and that's why we're having success.

Q: The 9/11 opener in 2022 against the Jets at MetLife was your first game as a pro.

A: During the anthem they have the flag covering the entire field, there's someone singing the anthem and then the mic cuts out. And when the mic cut out, everybody in the stands started singing. It gave me goose bumps. I don't get goose bumps that often, but it was really cool. Haven't had a moment cooler than that since.

Ravens kicker Justin Tucker, right, kicks a field goal held by punter Jordan Stout against the Bills on Jan. 19, 2025, in Orchard Park, N.Y. AP Photo/Adrian Kraus

Q: Do you remember where you were on 9/11?

A: I was 3 years old. I think I have a memory of the phone ringing and my grandfather never got worked up, but when he answered the phone I saw it on his face, that something was wrong. That's all I remember. Coulda been a dream. But I think that's what it was.

A: I grew too fast when I was young. I played soccer. Because of the Osgood-Schlatter, I had to stop playing soccer and start kicking footballs because I couldn't keep up with the pace of the game.

Q: Were you upset that you had to give up soccer?

A: Absolutely at the time. Blessing in disguise, though. God had a plan.

Q: Why did you cut your hair when it was longer?

A: At one point I felt like I needed an image to stand out, but then I decided I just wanted my performance to do it, so I cut it. I wanted to be marketable, and it worked in college [Penn State], but then I got in the NFL and I wasn't good enough, so it didn't matter anyway. It was annoying, so I cut it.

A: I love going out and getting, like, the best food in the world.

A: Cristiano Ronaldo, Tom Brady, Ray Guy.

A: 'Cause he was the best at his position of all time. And same with Cristiano Ronaldo. And then same with Ray Guy as a punter.

A: There's this guy on YouTube I like, his [user]name is Markiplier and he just plays video games. His name is Mark Fischbach.

Q: You're the NFL's highest-paid punter — three years, $12.3 million.

A: Oh, no. Hypothetically it should, but it doesn't at all, and that's because becoming the highest-paid now I feel like nobody looks at me as less than I'm supposed to be the best, and everyone talks to me like I am the best. So I feel like there's no pressure, it's like I go out there, I play, the GM loves me, think my coaches love me, the team loves me. It's not just because of performance, it's because of how I come in and work every day, the charisma and energy I bring, I guess. I feel like I haven't been more supported by a staff and a team in my career.

Q: What do you mean by the charisma you bring?

A: I'm just a positive guy. I bring a lot of good positive energy. Good or bad.

Q: You were a walk-on at Virginia Tech and became an All-Pro. How proud of yourself are you?

A: It starts out small, you're playing high school ball, you want to be all-state in Virginia. And then you get all-state and you want to go play Division I ball. You get a walk-on spot, and you want a scholarship and then you earn a scholarship. Once you get the scholarship you want to be an All-American. Then you become All-American, and then you want to get drafted. And then you get drafted. The pinnacle of this is All-Pro. So once I got All-Pro and Pro Bowl, I kind of felt like for the first time in my entire sports career, like "OK, I did it." And now, at this point, where I am now, I don't want to just be All-Pro now, I want to be the best. So that's the new goal. Until the career's over with, no one can say definitively if I'm the best or not, so it's gonna be never-ending.

Q: How about the doubters that you endured your first three years in Baltimore?

#2905. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2904. NY Giants’ pass defense has been much better than expected

There were, if not outright concerns, certainly questions about how good the New York Giants defense could be in 2026.

Between edge defenders Brian Burns, Abdul Carter, and Kayvon Thibodeaux, as well as off-ball linebackers Tremaine Edmunds, Arvell Reese, and Micah McFadden, the Giants' linebacking corps was expected to be one of the best in the NFL. And the hope was that their defensive line would at least be serviceable after the addition of veteran defensive tackles D.J. Reader and Shelby Harris.

The bigger questions were about the Giants' secondary, particularly after Cor'Dale Flott left in free agency.

2025 free agents Paulson Adebo and Jevon Holland hadn't panned out in their first years as Giants, while 2023 first round pick Deonte Banks had regressed badly under DC Shane Bowen and was considered a bust. The Giants signed former Cleveland Browns and Jacksonville Jaguars cornerback Greg Newsome II, as well as former Baltimore Ravens safety Ar'Darius Washington, in the 2026 free agency period and drafted Tennessee cornerback Colton Hood in the second round of the draft.

However, those moves weren't viewed as much more than Band-Aids for a Giants' secondary, and national commentors suggested that the Giants' scondary could be putrid in the first year of John Harbaugh's rebuild.

Fast-forward nine games, and the Giants' secondary has been much, much, better than anticipated under Dennard Wilson, Donald D'Alesio, and Addison Lynch.

The Giants are currently fielding the NFL's fourth-best defense in total yardage allowed (2,751 total yards). They're third-best in passing yardage allowed per game (184.1 yards per game), second in yards per completion allowed (9.8 yards per completion), and 2nd in yards per attempt allowed (6.2 yards per attempt).

The Giants are accomplishing this feat with smothering coverage from their starting corners. Both Deonte Banks and Greg Newsome II have been incredibly sticky in man coverage, allowing little separation for receivers.

Banks is allowing just 2.1 yards of separation as the nearest defender, per NFL NextGenStats, while Newsome is allowing all of 2.6 yards of separation. Banks and Newsome are both forcing tight window throws on over 40 percent of the plays in which they're the closest defender — which is exactly what the "step coverage" Dennard Wilson's defense calls for.

(note: "step coverage" calls for the defensive back to be within a step of the offensive player)

And while slot corner Dru Phillips is allowing more separation (3.6 yards on average), he's allowing -5.9 EPA in coverage and a Catch Rate Over Expected of -13.0 percent.

Defensive coordinator Dennard Wilson praised the Giants' safeties when he spoke to the media on Thursday, saying,

"I think Ar'Darius played his best game the last outing against Washington. Obviously, he tackled extremely well. You saw him, in the fourth quarter in a critical situation, come up with an interception. He's been doing everything that I know he could do, from being around him in Baltimore when he was there. He is a smaller guy that plays with a big stick. He is going to attack the line of scrimmage. He tackles well. He can cover. So he's doing a good job. I think all the safeties are doing a good job in their role. It's kind of safety by committee. All of them deserve the opportunity to play. So that's why we've been playing them."

And Wilson wasn't just puffing up his players. Each of the Giants' safeties have played very well in coverage as well.

Jevon Holland has been among the best safeties in the NFL this year, allowing a -27.8 percent catch rate over expected which is 3rd best among all DBs in the NFL and -13.4 EPA allowed. Jason Pinnock has proven to be remarkably sticky in coverage

The Giants' airtight coverage is allowing them to lead the NFL in the rate of Cover-0, that is man coverage across the board, at 10.8%. That jumps to 11.8% in obvious passing situations, and the Giants are sixth in EPA allowed in those situations.

Interestingly, the Giants' pressure rate has been remarkably low when only rushing four defenders, even with their sticky coverage. That's led to frustrations from fans when it comes to sack totals (or the lack thereof), particularly with regard to Abdul Carter and Kayvon Thibodeaux. Both of the Giants' starting EDGEs have elite get-offs, but have all of 2.0 sacks between them.

NextGenStats has shown us that part of the problem is that the Giants' opponents specifically game plan for their hyper-athletic pass rushers. The Giants' opponents feature the second-fastest Time To Throw of any defense in the NFL, as well as a Top-3 deviation from their usual Time To Throw.

The Giants' sticky Cover-0 packages work with Wilson's aggressive blitz patterns (as he refers to them) to provide an answer.

The Giants are now second only to the Minnesota Vikings — and the madman that is Brian Flores — in the average number of pass rushers sent at 4.90.

The Giants are currently sitting at 5-3-1 on the season, and they can thank their defense for closing out four of those wins, as well as forcing the tie against the Washington Commanders.

All told, the Giants' secondary has been one of the most surprising units in the entire NFL, and it's powering a defense that is finally playing up to its potential. I suspect they'd be getting much more attention on the national level it weren't for the low sack numbers and what Flores is doing in Minnesota.

Now, the Giants will need them to keep it up, or perhaps even find another level if they can.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2903. Georgia’s Kirby Smart Calls Out SEC Scheduling Ahead of South Carolina Showdown

OXFORD, MISSISSIPPI - NOVEMBER 07: Kirby Smart of the Georgia Bulldogs reacts during the first half against the Mississippi Rebels at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium on November 07, 2026 in Oxford, Mississippi. (Photo by Justin Ford/Getty Images) Getty

ATHENS, GEORGIA - SEPTEMBER 26: Head coach Kirby Smart of the Georgia Bulldogs reacts during the fourth quarter against the Oklahoma Sooners at Sanford Stadium on September 26, 2026 in Athens, Georgia. (Photo by Todd Kirkland/Getty Images) Getty

The Georgia Bulldogs have another difficult SEC road test waiting for them Saturday night, and head coach Kirby Smart made an interesting observation about the conference's scheduling ahead of the matchup.

GettyOXFORD, MISSISSIPPI – NOVEMBER 07: Kirby Smart of the Georgia Bulldogs reacts during the first half against the Mississippi Rebels at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium on November 07, 2026 in Oxford, Mississippi. (Photo by Justin Ford/Getty Images)

Georgia (9-1, 7-1 SEC) travels to Columbia to face South Carolina (6-4, 4-4) at Williams-Brice Stadium. The game kicks off at 7:30 p.m. ET on ABC, giving the Gamecocks an opportunity to host the nation's top-ranked team under the lights.

During an interview with SEC Network reporter Marion Crowder, Smart was asked about the atmosphere Georgia should expect. His response included a pointed observation about how frequently the Bulldogs have been scheduled for difficult nighttime road games.

"Noise, noise and more noise," Smart said. "They do a great job here. It's a great atmosphere. For some reason, the SEC likes putting us on the road at night against really good teams. We've had three of them this year, and this one will be no different."

The comments were interesting because Smart wasn't simply discussing South Carolina's home-field advantage. He also appeared to be questioning why Georgia keeps finding itself in these difficult situations.

Saturday marks Georgia's third nighttime road game of the season. The Bulldogs won their previous two, demonstrating their ability to handle environments that can overwhelm less experienced teams.

However, Smart's observation raises a fair question about the challenges Georgia has faced compared with programs that have enjoyed more opportunities to play important games at home.

Of course, difficult road environments are part of competing in the SEC. Georgia's success under Smart has been built around handling those challenges rather than using them as excuses.

Still, the timing of his comments makes the matchup more interesting.

South Carolina enters Saturday with six victories, but its record doesn't necessarily tell the entire story. Playing at Williams-Brice Stadium under the lights presents a different challenge than facing the Gamecocks in a quieter setting.

Smart acknowledged that reality earlier in the week, emphasizing the importance of preparing for communication problems and unpredictable situations on the road.

GettyATHENS, GEORGIA – SEPTEMBER 26: Head coach Kirby Smart of the Georgia Bulldogs reacts during the fourth quarter against the Oklahoma Sooners at Sanford Stadium on September 26, 2026 in Athens, Georgia. (Photo by Todd Kirkland/Getty Images)

Georgia also has to account for South Carolina quarterback LaNorris Sellers, whose athleticism and ability to extend plays have earned considerable praise from Smart.

The Bulldogs have won four consecutive meetings against South Carolina, including a 24-14 victory in 2023. Nevertheless, Saturday represents their first meeting since that season, adding another interesting element to the rivalry.

For Georgia, the objective remains straightforward. The Bulldogs want to finish their SEC schedule with another victory and strengthen their position entering the postseason.

But Smart's comments highlight something that can get overlooked when discussing championship contenders.

Winning nine games is impressive. Doing it while repeatedly traveling into hostile environments at night requires another level of preparation.

Georgia has handled those challenges before. Saturday night will reveal whether the Bulldogs can do it again.

#2902. NBA Cup 2026: James Harden puts up 51 in Cavs' win over Pacers; shorthanded Raptors fend off Pistons, end 6-game skid

James Harden played by far his best game in a Cleveland Cavaliers uniform on Friday night during NBA Cup group play.

The 37-year-old guard erupted for 51 points in a 128-119 home win over the Indiana Pacers, a season high and his most since joining the Cavaliers amid the 2025-26 campaign.

Cleveland (9-8) improved to 2-1 in Cup competition, knocking on the Miami Heat's door atop East Group B. Indiana (5-11), which trailed the Cavaliers by as many as 24 points, dropped to 1-2 in this year's NBA Cup.

Harden shot 15 of 26 from the floor, including 7 of 11 from beyond the arc. He also finished 14 of 14 from the charity stripe and with six rebounds and six assists. The one-time NBA MVP and 11-time All-Star now has the third-most 50-plus-point games in NBA history. With 26 such performances, he passed the late Kobe Bryant (25) on Friday and now trails Michael Jordan (31) and Wilt Chamberlain (118) in that category.

While collecting his first 50-piece with the Cavaliers, Harden moved into ninth all-time for most combined NBA regular and postseason points. His handles were on full display. He rained down 3s, and he made an impact at the rim.

Toronto came into Friday missing a trio of starters and on a six-game losing streak. Darko Rajaković's team was playing without RJ Barrett, Collin Murray-Boyles and, of course, Kawhi Leonard, who's still working his way back from a knee injury.

And yet, the shorthanded Raptors (6-10) found a way on the road versus the Detroit Pistons (10-5) in NBA Cup action, ending their skid with a riveting 105-102 win.

It's important to note that Detroit isn't at full strength, either. Jalen Duren remains sidelined with a hamstring issue. Plus, Duncan Robinson is out with a quad strain.

The absence of some star power didn't diminish what turned out to be an incredible matchup between two squads in East Group A and between a pair of former Montverde Academy teammates. Toronto's Scottie Barnes and Detroit's Cade Cunningham went toe-to-toe, particularly down the stretch.

Cunningham, who clocked out with 23 points and nine assists, hit a go-ahead 3-pointer over Barnes with less than 20 seconds remaining. Then, with the Raptors down one point, Barnes drove on Cunningham and reclaimed the lead.

Cunningham tried to answer back but ended up with nowhere to go with the ball after picking up his dribble en route to the basket. He passed it, and Toronto rookie Allen Graves nabbed a steal that helped wrap up the victory.

Barnes had 29 points, 5 rebounds and 5 assists. Graves, a first-round pick from Santa Clara making his first career start, piled up 20 points, 10 rebounds and 3 steals.

After that nail-biter, Toronto is 2-1 in East Group A, whereas Detroit is 1-1. The bottom dweller of the group, the Milwaukee Bucks (0-2 in NBA Cup play), was eliminated from knockout-round contention because the Raptors and Orlando Magic both won on Friday.

The Magic left Brooklyn victorious 113-111, thanks to Franz Wagner, who scored 35 points on 13-of-25 shooting and knocked down a game-winning jumper with 2.4 ticks left against the Nets.

Orlando is 3-0 in NBA Cup games this year.

Note: * denotes teams that have been eliminated from knockout round contention.

#2901. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.
