#2897. James Harden erupts for 51 to pass Kobe Bryant on 50-point games list

James Harden turned back the clock Friday, Nov. 20, reminding NBA opponents and fans why he is one of the best scorers in league history.

The 37-year-old scored 51 points Friday to lead the Cavaliers to a 128-119 win over the Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup East Group B game at Rocket Arena in Cleveland.

Harden's 51 points were the most he's scored in a game this year and came two days shy of the one-year anniversary of his last 50-point game, when, as a member of the Los Angeles Clippers, he shelled out 55 in a win against the Charlotte Hornets on Nov. 22, 2025.

But his scoring output Friday night represented a more meaningful milestone: Harden notched his 26th 50-point game of his career, passing Kobe Bryant for third all time in NBA history. Michael Jordan ranks second with 31 such games, while the all-time leader, Wilt Chamberlain, seems likely to retain that record for a very long time having crossed the 50-point threshold an astounding 118 times.

Only three other players have scored at least 50 points this season, and all three are MVP candidates. Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić has passed the 50-point mark twice, tallying 53 against the Portland Trail Blazers on Nov. 5 and 52 against the Utah Jazz on Oct. 25. San Antonio Spurs star Victor Wembanyama dropped 52 against the Dallas Mavericks on Nov. 4, while Detroit Pistons guard Cade Cunningham scored 52 in a double overtime win against the Washington Wizards on Nov. 12.

Harden ranks ninth in NBA history and 10th in NBA-ABA history for most career points with 29,633 in 1,237 games played. Next on the career list is Hall of Famer Julius Erving with 30,026 career points in the NBA and ABA. Philadelphia 76ers star LeBron James holds the record for most points in league history with 43,628 (through Nov. 20).

#2896. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2895. Are NY Mets Secretly Among Freddy Peralta Free Agent Suitors? Potential Evidence Emerges

A new report may open the door to the New York Mets possibly pursuing a reunion with the man they traded before the August MLB trade deadline, Freddy Peralta.

After a historic collapse in 2025 and a disastrous last-place showing this past season, Mets President David Stearns is under immense pressure to get the club back to the winning ways that fans saw just two years ago. If there was one clear message he should have taken away from the last two seasons, it's that you can never have enough proven starting pitching.

It is a major reason why the Mets are viewed as the current favorite to sign superstar pitcher Tarik Skubal in free agency. However, it is possible they lose the chase for the two-time Cy Young Award winner to the Los Angeles Dodgers or Toronto Blue Jays. So Stearns and his staff must have secondary options to pivot to in the event that did happen.

Padres veteran Michael King is one player often linked to the Mets as a star to pair with young ace Nolan McLean in 2027 and beyond. Some around the team have even suggested that a reunion with Clay Holmes, despite trading him before the deadline, can't be ruled out. But what if New York was targeting a different reunion?

Oct 26, 2026; St. Petersburg, Florida, USA; Tampa Bay Rays pitcher Freddy Peralta (51) reacts against the Milwaukee Brewers during the fifth inning in game three of the 2026 World Series at Tropicana Field. Mandatory Credit: Kim Klement Neitzel-Imagn Images

On Saturday, The Athletic's Ken Rosenthal and Will Sammon reported that "Half a dozen or so teams" are part of the free agent market for Peralta. The MLB insiders claim that the team he helped lead to the World Series this fall, the Tampa Bay Rays, the Chicago White Sox, the Padres, the Chicago Cubs, the Blue Jays, and two other unknown clubs are the contenders for the pitcher's services.

All of those teams make sense and are in need of starting pitching this offseason. But who could the two "unknown" teams be? The Colorado Rockies could certainly be one, since ESPN's Jeff Passan reported this week that the Rockies badly want pitching and are willing to pay "at the sort of price that compensates [free agents] properly for the peril of starting half of their games at Coors Field."

The Baltimore Orioles could be the other after rumors have suggested they are being aggressive to improve the roster and are open to adding big contracts. However, after that, there are not many other teams that will be willing to pay what Peralta is likely to get in free agency. That is projected to be anywhere from $80 million to $140 million in a multi-year deal. And that number could go up substantially with so many teams interested.

At first glance, the Mets reuniting with Peralta seems extremely unlikely. They gave up a strong prospect package in a trade for him in the winter, and he rewarded them by going 5-9 in 22 starts with a 4.99 ERA. It is understandable why they traded the impending free agent in August.

Jul 20, 2026; Milwaukee, Wisconsin, USA; New York Mets pitcher Freddy Peralta (51) during the game against the Milwaukee Brewers at American Family Field. Mandatory Credit: Jeff Hanisch-Imagn Images

But there is also no denying how good he was after the move to Tampa. The 30-year-old went 5-2 with a 3.25 ERA in the second half. Then, outside of a rough start in the World Series, he was great in the playoffs, including a 2-0 record with a 0.75 ERA in the ALCS that earned him series MVP. Based on his nine-year career, it does seem like his first half in New York was an outlier.

After the deal, he had nothing but praise for his time in New York, and the two-time All-Star is one of the most well-liked players in the game.

"Multiple players have called him the 'best teammate' they have ever had," FanSided's Robert Murray reported this week. "Others have praised his consistency, with Peralta posting an ERA ranging between 2.70-3.99 in every year from 2020-2025. And others pointed to his ability to bounce back after a rough first half with the Mets."

Is a reunion likely? Probably not. However, if the Mets fail to sign Skubal, they still badly need proven pitching. There are not a lot of great options available outside of King. Since he would cost far less than Skubal and still has a pretty good career track record, Peralta re-signing can't be completely ruled out if they are also open to a Clay Holmes reunion.

#2894. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2892. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

#2891. Brooklyn Nets respond with a fight – but not a win – in 113-111 heartbreaker vs Magic

There is a funny irony to sports, especially the NBA.

You can spend hours and days dissecting what's wrong with a team, questioning everyone from the top of the organization down. The roster, the direction, and everything in between. And then, two nights later, the team goes out and completely contradicts the narrative, for better or worse.

After getting embarrassed by 30 points against the Chicago Bulls on Wednesday, the Nets entered Friday night's matchup against the Orlando Magic with expectations as low as they've been this season. The squad entered with a 3-11 record, with five of their losses coming by 15+ points, the most by any team in the league this season.

Yet, somehow, 48 hours later, the Nets looked like an entirely different basketball team … for the better.

The energy was palpable, the defensive effort was there, players were diving for loose balls, and the team looked noticeably better.

Danny Wolf, getting the start over Egor Dëmin, delivered 20 points and four 3-pointers. Julius Randle added 27 points, nine rebounds, and six assists while Mikel Brown Jr. contributed 18 points off the bench, tied for his career-high.

And for a while, it looked like the Nets might actually have something to celebrate. Instead, they were reminded of another cruel reality of basketball: you could do a lot right and still lose games.

Despite overcoming an early 13-point deficit and taking a fourth-quarter lead, Brooklyn fell to Orlando, 113-111, on a Franz Wagner game-winner with under three seconds to play. Wagner finished with 35 points. It could have been a signature win for the team, but instead, it ended in heartbreaking fashion.

With 2.4 seconds left, the Nets got the ball back and had a chance to seal it, but Julius Randle missed an open game-winning 3-pointer.

"Julius had the ball in his hands… that's what I wanted," said Jordi Fernandez post-game. "He shot a step back and it was wide open, and I live with it,"

The game shouldn't have even come down to that moment, though. Unfortunately for the Brooklyn Nets, there isn't a standing for moral victories, and they now fall to 3-12.

The Nets jumped out to a 17-11 lead in the first quarter before Orlando started to catch up. By the end of the first frame, the score had flipped, with the Magic up 38-25.

Brooklyn's biggest early problem was stopping the Magic from shooting from deep. Without strong perimeter defense, which the Nets have lacked all season, it's going to stay tough for them to beat teams with more roster talent, like Orlando.

To the team's credit, though, there was a clear increase in effort and pride, which had been in question over the past few games. Perhaps the "internal discussions," as Michael Porter Jr. described them, after Wednesday's blowout sent a message.

From Julius Randle to Mikel Brown Jr., everyone looked to be giving their all early on, diving for loose balls, and everything that comes with being a high-motor team even if they were trailing.

Subsequently, the Nets started to catch up. With under eight minutes to play in the second quarter, Brooklyn cut the score to 42-38 after a strong run fueled by Michael Porter Jr. MPJ, who scored 14 points in his first 13 minutes of the night after finishing with just five points in 27 minutes against the Bulls on Wednesday night.

Fernández also made a change to his starting lineup on Friday night. Danny Wolf replaced Egor Dëmin, who had been in a rough patch entering the game. It also marked his first time coming off the bench since November of last season. That decision worked out, as Wolf thrived while Dëmin's struggles continued, scoring six points in 21 minutes on 1-of-6 shooting, including a 0-of-3 mark from 3-point range.

"We were struggling with rebounding and with our defense, and I felt like Danny was playing well," Fernandez said, adding. "Obviously, there's no guarantees or anything for anybody."

Entering halftime, the Nets trailed just 62-61, chipping away at the 13-point deficit they faced at the end of the first frame. It was a battle from beyond the arc, as Orlando continued to find success from deep, hitting a whopping 57.1% of their tries, while Brooklyn converted 41.2% of their 3-point attempts.

The Nets also did a strong job of forcing the Magic into mistakes, forcing 12 turnovers in the first half compared to their eight. The team also capitalized on the extra possessions, scoring 18 points off turnovers.

Coming out of halftime, the Nets stayed in the game but didn't do enough to take the lead entering the fourth quarter, still trailing 91-88. Nolan Traoré left the game with a hamstring injury during the third frame and was quickly ruled out, limping into the locker room.

Asked about whether Traore injury was serious, Fernandez responded: "I don't know."

Traoré has been a crucial piece in the Nets' backcourt this season, but when he went down, rookie Mikel Brown Jr. stepped up in a big way.

Brown logged 13 points in the third quarter, keeping the Nets in the game while hitting both of his shots from beyond the arc. By the end of the frame, he had logged 15 points in 18 minutes. Fernandéz sent him to the bench to begin the fourth quarter, giving Keon Ellis his first minutes of the night, before returning with just over six minutes remaining in the final frame.

Early on in the fourth quarter, the Nets grabbed their first lead since the first half. Wolf had a breakaway dunk and a crucial 3-pointer, marking his fourth deep make in moments, while Julius Randle hit his first three of the game. It brought the score to 98-93 with 8:35 left, forcing Orlando to use a timeout.

But as the Nets have learned plenty of times this season, building a lead and protecting one are two very different things. Orlando worked their way back, setting up a dramatic finish. And despite the Nets' improved play throughout the night, it was the Magic who closed out the game.

With the game tied 111 in the closing seconds, Wagner knocked down a contested fadeaway jumper from just inside the 3-point line with 2.4 seconds remaining, putting Orlando ahead by two. Brooklyn still had a chance to steal the game, but Randle's potential game-winning 3-pointer missed.

Just like that, the Nets had another loss to their record, falling 113-111.

Randle still led Brooklyn with 27 points, nine rebounds, six assists, and three steals, shooting 9-of-19 from the field. Day'Ron Sharpe also continued his strong start to the year, adding another double-double with 17 points and 11 rebounds.

Meanwhile, Wolf arguably had his best game as a pro, finishing with 20 points on 6-of-10 shooting overall, including 4-of-6 from deep. Post-game, he talked about his game.

"It's hard to come up here and keep having these moral victories," said Wolf. "There's been a lot of those this year. I think we responded very well after that Chicago game. But with how hard guys played, we really wanted that win and we needed that win."

Meanwhile, Noah Clowney's struggles continued, as he went scoreless on four field-goal attempts in 22 minutes and the Wagner game-winner came over him on defense.

The positive spin is that the Nets showed much more fight than they did two nights ago, but there's still no win to show for it.

Randle who excoriated his teammates for their effort during Wednesday's debacle saw some positives this time out.

"We competed our tails off. We played really hard. We were way more connected as a group on both ends of the floor," he said. "The game is won within the margin, so it's just little things, little possessions."

Although the Nets called up three roster players from the G League, Ben Saraf, Drake Powell and Joshua Jefferson, they didn't play, with only Saraf suiting up.

Final Score: Orlando Magic, 113, Brooklyn Nets, 111

Friday also marked the first matchup between the Wagner brothers: Franz and Mo. Safe to say Franz took home the family bragging rights.

With their parents looking on, Franz and Moe Wagner shared some salty but mostly emotional talk. The 6'10" 25-year-old Franz is four years younger than the 6'11" 29-year-old Moe.

"It's awkward, I'm not gonna lie. I don't really know how to compartmentalize it, really deal with it, and try to focus on the game plan and see what happens emotionally," Moe said pre-game. "It's a dilemma because you want him to do well. At the same time, you game plan for him, you prepare to limit him. So, it's an awkward feeling. I've never been in this position."

"My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him," Franz said. "But as weird as it's going to be, it's a blessing to share the court with your family.Not a lot of people get to do that, so that's how I look at it."

In the end, of course, it was Franz's game, scoring 35 points, bringing his season averages to 24.4 points, 8.5 assists and 6.9 rebounds per game while Moe, who went into the game as the NBA's leading 3-point shooter, put up three points.

"He's playing incredible," Moe said. "New coach, new system, found a way to be a playmaker and scorer at the same time, dominating the pace, the paint. Obviously with an enormous usage rate as well, which you've got to give him credit for the physicality he plays with … He's a joy to watch.

I wanted to win the game. It's one of the more conflicting games I've ever been a part of," Moe said afterward. "That is weird, but we've got enough problems going on. I can't worry about nothing on the other side. I'm trying to win the game, and that one hurts."

A lot of Nets fans would like to see the Wagner brother reunited in Brooklyn but that seems unlikely. Franz has become the face of the franchise in Orlando and is under contract through 2029-30 and is owed $185 million.

The Nets will have two days off before heading to Minnesota, where they will face the 11-5 Timberwolves on Monday. Tip-off is set for 7:30 p.m. ET.

It will be another uphill battle for the 3-12 Nets, who continue to spiral.

Monday will mark Julius Randle's first matchup against Minnesota since Brooklyn acquired him over the summer. It will also be Brooklyn's first of two nationally televised games this year.

#2890. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2889. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2887. Retirees face a looming deadline for required withdrawals — missing it could trigger a tax penalty

Every year, millions of Americans must take required withdrawals from retirement accounts by Dec. 31.

Amid the busy holiday season, it's easy to miss the year-end deadline for required minimum distributions — a mistake that can trigger a hefty IRS penalty, experts said.

"One of the most common RMD mistakes is waiting until the last minute," said certified financial planner Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth at Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon.

Most retirees must start RMDs from pretax accounts at age 73. The withdrawal is based on the previous year's account balances on Dec. 31, your age and an IRS "life expectancy factor."

The first annual RMD deadline is April 1 of the year after you turn 73, and future withdrawals are due by Dec. 31. If you wait until April 1 for the first one, you'll need two RMDs that year.

RMDs also apply to certain inherited IRAs if the original IRA owner reached RMD age before their death.

In 2023, more than 14.8 million taxpayers age 70 or older had a traditional individual retirement account, according to the latest IRS data. The average year-end market value of the accounts was about $330,000.

Deferring RMDs until the last minute raises the likelihood of mistakes, such as overlooking certain retirement accounts or making withdrawal calculation errors, Canellopoulos said.

Some 7% of Vanguard investors at RMD age missed their yearly withdrawal in 2025, up slightly from 6.7% in 2024, according to a Vanguard analysis of about 400,000 RMD-age retirees with IRA balances published in December 2025.

Among those investors, the average missed RMD was $10,500 in 2025, Vanguard told CNBC.

Investors who don't take RMDs by the annual deadline may owe an excise tax to the IRS, worth up to 25% of the amount they should have withdrawn. For example, the excise tax could be up to $2,625 for a $10,500 missed RMD.

If investors miss a required withdrawal, they could reduce the penalty to 10% if they "timely corrected" the RMD within two years and file Form 5329, according to the IRS.

In some cases, the IRS could waive the penalty entirely due to "reasonable error" if investors take "reasonable steps" to fix the shortfall, according to the agency. Investors can file Form 5329 with a letter of explanation to request the waiver.

"If you don't ask, you're not going to get it," said certified public accountant Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird. "I've never heard of anybody being denied the waiver."

Investors who give to charity may be skipping a specific donation strategy that can reduce or eliminate their annual RMDs, according to Lawrence Pon, a CFP and CPA based in Redwood City, California.

They can use a so-called qualified charitable distribution, or QCD, Pon said.

A QCD has a dual tax benefit: It doesn't count as taxable income and may also satisfy at least part of an investor's yearly RMD, Pon said.

The move requires a direct transfer from an investor's pretax IRA to an eligible nonprofit organization. They can donate up to $111,000 in 2026. While QCD eligibility begins at age 70½, transfers won't start counting toward RMDs until an investor reaches their RMD age.

Generally, taxpayers only get a tax break for large charitable donations if they itemize deductions on their tax returns. But QCDs still provide a tax benefit for charitable gifts when investors use the standard deduction.

QCDs "provide a real benefit if you are not itemizing [tax deductions]," said Pon.

While there's no tax deduction for QCDs, the transfer won't increase a taxpayer's adjusted gross income, or AGI. Raising AGI can cause other tax consequences, such as higher premiums for Medicare Part B and Part D.

Defense was stingy against the Steelers. We've seen some movement towards Cincinnati. We opened Bengals -4.5, it's up to -6.5. Total opened at 52.5, up to 54.5. A flood of money on Bengals money and over. Don't anticipate that changing in this one."

Gable: "No surprise, this has been straight Cincinnati money since we opened it. Opened Bengals -4, that was before the Sunday night game against Pittsburgh. We adjusted the rating, moved the number up. Anything less than -6.5, people have been laying on the Bengals. It's even public money at -6.5 on Cincinnati. Total has jumped from 51.5 at the open to 54 presently."

#2885. Lakers survive in ugly game to down Kings

Another sloppy game saw the Lakers struggle to the finish line before coming away victorious against the Kings on Friday, 106-104.

LA remained in a funk not dissimilar from Wednesday's loss to the Jazz, trailing by as many as 11 points. The bench helped spark a run in the third quarter to get back into the game.

Matisse Thybulle got off to a hot start with two triples. Everyone in the starting lineup, minus Ziarie Williams, scored a bucket. Keegan Murray and Domantas Sabonis combined for all 12 of Sacramento's first points.

LA struggled with turnovers early, committing four. Luka Dončić started scoring and was already close to double figures with nine points. Sabonis and Murray were still leading the Kings with eight points each.

At the end of the first, the game was tied. Los Angeles was shooting better than the Kings, 52% to 44%. Luka ended the quarter with 13 points.

Walker Kessler started the second period strong, scoring four points. Zach LaVine started to heat up for the Kings, scoring four points. It was Sabonis, though, who continued to keep Sacramento in the game with 15 points.

Quentin Grimes missed his first four shots in the half, but finally scored on a layup. He had six points, mostly thanks to free throws.

The game was tied again with six minutes left in the half.

Cameron Carr had an immediate impact off the bench, scoring five points. Los Angeles struggled with their offense. They were 7-20 from the field in the quarter. Sacramento went on a 9-0 scoring run.

As a result, the Kings were up by eight at halftime.

De'Andre Hunter scored first to start the third period for Sacramento. The Kings seemed to have all the momentum as Acuff Jr. also drained a triple. LA, though, had some life as they scored six in a row, forcing Sacramento to call a timeout.

As soon as it looked like the Lakers were gaining momentum, Sacramento struck back almost immediately. Thybulle drained a three to make it a one-point game before the Kings responded with a 6-0 run.

LA started gaining more defensive momentum thanks to Jarred Vanderbilt's extra effort plays. That included a sequence in which he got three rebounds in a row that resulted in Sandro Mamukelashvili converting on a three-point play.

At the end of the third, the purple and gold were only down two.

The final frame started with a 3-pointer from Kessler that received a nice crowd pop. Collin Sexton also began to take over offensively, scoring four points. Los Angeles had kicked up their defense, and in return, their offense was flowing more smoothly.

Thybulle knocked down his fourth triple of the game, giving the team a five-point lead with 7:36 left.

Williams finally got a 3-pointer to fall after several games of struggling from behind the arc. Sexton was now the only player off the bench in double figures with 10. Hunter continued his strong play for the Kings, converting on a three-point play. He had 25 points.

At the 3:30 mark, the purple and gold were up by five.

The teams swapped big shots to either tie or take the lead. Luka was poked in the eye by Murray, but remained in the game.

The Lakers went up by two after Grimes made a pair of free throws. Hunter responded with a jumper on the other end to tie it again.

With 1:12 left, Grimes knocked down a much-needed three to put the team up by three.

Sacramento missed a shot. Luka then had the ball and turned it over. The Lakers intentionally fouled up three to put LaVine on the line and he split the pair. Kessler was then fouled after rebounding the miss but missed both free throws.

LaVine had the opportunity to win with a deep three and missed.

Luka finished with 25 points, four rebounds and eight assists. Kessler ended with 15 points and three blocks. Thybulle pitched in with 14 points. Grimes logged 13 points with five rebounds and seven assists.

Carr had five points off the bench. Vanderbilt scored four points with four rebounds and two assists. Mamu finished with nine points, nine rebounds and two assists. Sexton and Jake LaRavia combined for 16 points.

The Lakers' next game will be against the Utah Jazz on Monday at 7:00 PM PT.

#2882. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

While the Dolphins did win the last time these two teams met, scoring a decisive 30-13 victory over Buffalo last November, much has changed since then.

Parker doesn't bode well for a struggling offensive line. Austin Jackson, Miami's starter at right tackle, is ranked No. 71 out of 72 qualifying tackles at Pro Football Focus. He's No. 69 in pass block grade and No. 65 in run block grade. Rousseau should be excited to see Jackson across from him this week.

The rookie is likely to start this week with veteran Dorian Williams out due to a calf injury he suffered in last week's win over the New York Jets. He's appeared on just 21% of Buffalo's defensive snaps this season, totaling 18 tackles, but he alreayd has two fumble recoveries to his name, as well. He's struggled a bit in pass coverage, so look for Miami to try to manipulate him through motion and play-action early. Will the Bills call veteran Shaq Thompson up from the practice squad to supplement the linebacker group this week? And, if they do, will Thompson eat into Alarms-Orr's playing time? We'll find out on Sunday, but look for No. 52 to make some big hits this week.

The second-year man has looked totally lost this season, as a rookie year that flashed some potential has given way to a second season where he has lost not only a shot at a starting gig, but really any shot at playing many meaningful snaps. Rookie Davison Igbinosun has far outplayed Hairston this season, but with Christian Benford out this week, Hairston has a chance to build back some trust with the coaching staff. Miami's passing game isn't exactly elite—it's not even league-average, as the Dolphins rank No. 31 in attempts, No. 29 in passing yards, and No. 29 in passing touchdowns—so this is a good time for Hairston to make a play or two. I think the ship has sailed, at least for now, where we look at Hairston as someone who should be playing when all of Buffalo's corners are healthy; however, it would be nice for him to have a week where he looks like someone who could play in a big spot if needed. #2876. Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2875. Former Rookie of the Year Luis Gil and 2023 All-Star Josiah Gray among 44 non-tender free agents

Former AL Rookie of the Year Luis Gil was cut loose by the New York Yankees, among 44 players who became free agents when their teams declined to offer 2027 contracts by Friday's tender deadline.

A 28-year-old right-hander, Gil was voted the American League's top rookie in 2024 after going 15-7 with a 3.50 ERA in 29 starts. With a four-seam fastball that averaged 96.6 mph, he struck out 171 in 151 2/3 innings but walked a major league-high 77.

Sidelined by a lat strain for much of 2025, he went 4-1 with a 3.32 ERA in 11 starts. Gil spent much of 2026 in the minors and was sidelined by right shoulder inflammation, going 1-2 with a 4.61 ERA in four starts and five relief appearances for the Yankees.

He had a $2,162,500 salary this year and would have been eligible for arbitration.

New York also chose not to offer a contract to reliever Fernando Cruz. The 36-year-old right-hander didn't pitch after Aug. 25 because of a partially torn ulnar collateral ligament. He said he would try to return from the injury without surgery.

Cruz went 4-6 with a 3.13 ERA and two saves in 61 appearances last season. He struck out 67 batters and walked 32 in 54 2/3 innings.

Washington right-hander Josiah Gray, a 2023 All-Star, also was cut loose. Gray, who turns 29 next month, was limited to two games in 2024 because of a torn ulnar collateral ligament, then was limited to three September appearances this year because of a right flexor strain. He had been designated for assignment on Tuesday.

Others non-tendered included St. Louis infielder Nolan Gorman, Cleveland outfielder/infielder/catcher David Fry, Minnesota outfielder Trevor Larnach and right-hander Bailey Ober, Seattle outfielder Luke Raley, Toronto outfielder Davis Schneider, San Diego left-hander JP Sears, Los Angeles Dodgers right-hander Jake Cousins, Tampa Bay right-hander Cole Sulser and Philadelphia outfielder Bryan De La Cruz.

All those players would have been eligible for arbitration had they been offered contracts but instead joined a market that included 183 players who became free agents after the World Series.

Among players facing the tender deadline, first baseman/outfielder Gavin Sheets agreed to an $11 million, three-year contract with San Diego that allows him to earn $1.5 million annually in performance bonuses for plate appearances. The 2028 and 2029 seasons are $4 million player options that give him the right to terminate the deal and become a free agent.

Thirty-five players agreed to one-year contracts:

Baltimore right-hander Felix Bautista ($2.3 million).

Boston second baseman Romy Gonzalez ($1.65 million plus $25,000 performance bonuses each for 300 and 350 plate appearances), infielder Andrew Monasterio ($1.4 million), outfielder Eli White ($1,325,000 plus $25,000 performance bonuses each for 200, 250 and 300 plate appearances) and second baseman Jahmai Jones (the higher of $950,000 or the 2027 minimum plus $150,000).

Chicago Cubs catcher Joey Bart ($2.53 million).

Cleveland right-handers Colin Holderman ($2,175,000) and Matt Festa ($1.5 million), and outfielder Blake Perkins (non-guaranteed at the higher of $950,000 or the 2027 minimum plus $150,000, with a $500,000 salary while in the minor leagues).

#2874. Cardinals QB issues continue: Beck or Johnson to start?

During training camp this year, both the quarterback and receiver rooms were overflowing with talent and capable bodies. Now, the pass catchers are backups to backups, while the QB room is down to two rookies and a guy who is being paid by his 17th NFL club.

This weekend as the Arizona Cardinals are preparing to play the Kansas City Chiefs, one of the best teams in the AFC, the coaching staff just might be starting its fourth quarterback this season. Rookie Carson Beck may not start.

RELATED: SHOULD THE CARDINALS MAKE A CLAIM FOR QB DANIEL JONES?

Beck started his first NFL contest last week in the 34-6 beatdown from division foe Los Angeles Rams. He was scheduled to start again this weekend as center Hjalte Froholdt has been released from concussion protocol.

But Beck has had some flare-ups with back spasms. Although the team hasn't ruled Beck out for the Chiefs game, journeyman Josh Johnson is being prepped to start instead. If Johnson does play, that will become four starting quarterbacks in four games. What a QB turnstile.

Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur stated that the team is taking Beck's situation one minute at a time, which made it sound serious since he avoided the classic "one day at a time" standard answer. Beck did not participate in practice on Friday while the coaching staff is waiting to see how the young signalcaller responds to treatment. The rookie QB was previously sidelined with back spasms earlier in the season.

"We're not gonna shut the door on him, but we're gonna proceed as if Josh [Johnson] is gonna be starting on Sunday."

Beck was taken in Round 3 of this year's NFL draft and basically was the emergency quarterback for the majority of the year. Then Jacoby Brissett was shipped off, and suddenly Beck was the backup to Gardner Minshew against the Seattle Seahawks. Minshew became injured in that game and then was placed on IR after surgery to repair his () in his throwing arm.

Which meant, the next man up. Beck started against the Rams as the Cardinals offensive line did not provide much protection and sacked Beck five times, which could just as easily been double digit numbers. Beck finished going 11 for 18 for 108 yards and two picks, of which one was the receiver's fault.

Johnson is 40-years old. He was a fifth-round selection of the Tampa Bay Buccaneers way back in 2008. The Cardinals are his 17th NFL team. He has played for three clubs twice, plus was a roster member of an UFL team, an AAF franchise, and the XFL where he was the passer rating leader. He played in five games for Washington in 2025 with two starts.

Beck was seven years old when Johnson was drafted by Tampa Bay. Johnson has always been the backup, or the backup to the backup. He has played in just 50 NFL games with a mere 11 starts. His total career passing yards is 2,669, what most starters eclipse in a single season. Johnson has a career 58.7% completion ratio with 94 rushing attempts for 481 yards, so he is mobile.

Which means he has experience. If Beck can't go, rookie Joey Aguilar will become the backup with WR/KR Devin Duvernay the emergency QB.

#2873. Quentin Grimes' late three-pointer lifts Lakers to narrow win over Kings

The Lakers just finished a stretch during which they played four games in six days, a stretch that included back-to-back games this week, a stretch in which their mental and physical fortitude was tested.

They were not able to hold up during Wednesday night's defeat to the Utah Jazz, but the Lakers were able to bounce back with a 106-104 win over the hapless Sacramento Kings on Friday night at Crypto.com Arena behind another winning shot from Quentin Grimes.

Even though the Lakers were facing one-win Sacramento, they had to fight to the final few precious seconds to improve to 14-3 and 2-1 in NBA Cup play. They only secured the win after guard Zach LaVine missed a three-pointer just before the buzzer.

Read more: How the Lakers' Quentin Grimes wants to maintain his hot shooting, even off the bench

Lakers coach JJ Redick said he had some choice words for his players at halftime.

"I feel I did a decent job of regulating at halftime. They may have a differing opinion of that," Redick said. "Can't repeat every word I used. The main message was, 'We've got to be tougher. We've got to bring a little bit of energy. Certain nights, whether it's the team, how we're playing, the atmosphere in the arena, you can just be a little flat.' And I thought we just had to be tougher and pick up our energy. They accepted the challenge."

The score was tied 103-103 when the Lakers forced Kings rookie guard Darius Acuff Jr. into a turnover.

Grimes then hit a step-back three with 56.3 seconds left to give the Lakers a 106-103 lead.

It marked the second time this week Grimes made a winning three, the first coming off a banked-in trey Sunday at Phoenix with 32.7 seconds left — the day he said, "I called game."

Lakers center Walker Kessler scores off an offensive rebound against the Sacramento Kings on Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

"I think so," Grimes said, smiling. "Luckily, didn't have to bank it in, it just kind of went in pretty good. But yeah, it feels good to get another one of those games in, for sure."

Grimes didn't have the best shooting night. He was two for seven from the field, one for five from three-point range. Grimes missed his first three-pointer in the fourth quarter.

But he was unfazed with the game on the line, knocking down a 26-foot jumper.

"Yeah, it don't matter. [You] shoot the next one. I mean, [it's a] make-or-miss league," said Grimes, who scored a career-high 47 points against Charlotte on Tuesday. "You gonna make it, you gonna miss it. Don't matter if you go 0 for 10 or you can go 10 for 10. Next one, get it up, and then luckily that was the biggest shot in the game and the shot of the night."

After Acuff missed a three-point attempt, Luka Doncic turned the ball over, giving the basketball back to the Kings (1-14) with 23 seconds left.

Lakers star Luka Doncic drives to the basket against Sacramento Kings guard Zach LaVine (8) and forward Keegan Murray in the first half Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

LaVine made one of two free throws to pull the Kings to within 106-104.

Then Walker Kessler, who had 15 points and seven rebounds, missed two free throws with 5.3 seconds left, leaving the door open for the Kings.

But LaVine missed his three-point try as time expired, allowing the Lakers to escape on a night when Doncic had 25 points and Matisse Thybulle had 14.

So, it appeared that whatever Redick spewed at his team at halftime resonated.

"Yeah, I mean he yells," Doncic said. "But I'm used to it. So, got us all fired up. So, sometimes you get to yell."

Austin Reaves missed his fourth consecutive game because of a left heel bruise, but Redick said his star guard is making progress. The Lakers next play Monday night at home against the Utah Jazz and then again Friday at San Antonio. The hope, Redick said, is to get Reaves back for one of those games. Redick said Reaves had a "great day" on the court Friday and "made a lot of progress."

"We're trying to manage his pain," Redick said. "So, hopefully he'll be back in one of the next two games."

#2872. How China is using summits to expand its global influence

SHENZHEN, China — For a second year in a row, Chinese President Xi Jinping has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores.

U.S. President Donald Trump had a trilateral meeting with Xi and Putin on Thursday, after the three met the prior evening during a welcome banquet for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen. It marked the first time they had met together in person.

In September 2025, Xi, Putin and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi briefly laughed together for a friendly photo-op alongside an annual meeting of the Shanghai Cooperation Organization in Tianjin, China. The gathering followed easing tensions between Beijing and New Delhi.

China's "ability to engage directly with the world major powers" reflects the country's influence across the Global South and Eurasia, said Jodie Wen, postdoctoral fellow at the Center for International Security and Strategy at Tsinghua University. "China hopes to play a more constructive role in this conflict-[ridden], unstable world."

In the future, "China will hope to provide more platforms" within its borders for countries to discuss issues, she said.

Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

China has increasingly played the role of broker, for instance, providing the forum for restoring diplomatic ties between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023.

By participating in and hosting different multilateral organizations, China has stayed in the Western orbit while cultivating relations with non-Western countries. The two photo-ops last year and this year reflect only a portion of Xi's large number of meetings with different world leaders.

Xi has hosted more than 40 heads of state so far this year, including at least eight around the APEC meetings in Shenzhen this week. That's on top of receiving over 60 state visits in 2025, according to CNBC analysis of official Chinese readouts.

Domestic Chinese media have highlighted how Xi has met with many foreign leaders around the China-hosted events.

"Beijing sees these multilateral platforms as opportunities to expand its international influence and demonstrate its commitment to multilateralism, while bilateral diplomacy remains equally important," said Yue Su, principal economist for China at EIU.

But she noted that different countries increasingly pursue different geopolitical and economic priorities, making it challenging to reach consensus even through multilateral organizations.

"Against this backdrop, China's diplomatic priorities are increasingly focused on delivering tangible economic benefits through trade, investment, financial connectivity and technology cooperation," she said.

On Thursday, Xi announced at the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting that China would provide $20 million in support for the Asia Pacific region. He also advocated for the development of an "Asia-Pacific community with a shared future," an iteration of his commonly used slogan on a "shared future" for humanity more broadly.

The Shenzhen summit can create meaningful outcomes for crisis management, as well as diplomatic space for future negotiations, Tsinghua University's Wen said. She added that it's important for leaders to come together in person in order to avoid misunderstandings and further tensions.

Trump said Thursday at the trilateral that the U.S., China and Russia are "uniquely positioned" to discuss reducing nuclear weapons at their historic meeting. A Chinese readout subsequently said the three leaders discussed Ukraine, the Middle East and the Korean Peninsula.

—CNBC's Matthew Tan contributed to this report.

#2871. Steve Sarkisian reveals what’s led to Arch Manning’s success with Texas offense getting rolling

Steve Sarkisian reveals what's led to Arch Manning's success with Texas offense getting rolling appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

With the Texas football team heading into a SEC conference game Saturday against Arkansas, Longhorns head coach Steve Sarkisian is opening up about the squad. Sarkisian is revealing the key to what has unlocked Texas' offense this season.

That key revolves around the Longhorns quarterback, Arch Manning.

"We've had to use Arch's legs more than maybe we would have liked," Sarkisian said, per SEC Network.

#2870. Saints vs. Bears: Key Matchups

The New Orleans Saints are walking into Chicago as double-digit underdogs against the Bears. The Saints have dropped two of their last three, and their season has quickly been derailed.

Now in desperate need of a win, New Orleans will face their former head coach and current Bears defensive coordinator, Dennis Allen, in a game they were completely outmatched in just a year ago. If the Saints want to steal a win, it will be by taking advantage of these key matchups.

Saints fans will be the first ones to tell you about Allen's capabilities as a defensive playcaller. The former Saints head coach was known for his ability to call up exotic looks at the line of scrimmage, something that has become even more relevant this season with the massive success of Brian Flores and the Minnesota Vikings defense.

Tyler Shough has been a bright spot for the Saints this season, but is coming off one of the more disappointing games of his career. Even worse for Shough and the Saints, this was against a lackluster Carolina Panthers defense. Now New Orleans will be facing a team that is top-10 in sacks and just made a massive trade for one of the best defensive linemen of the decade, Jeffrey Simmons.

The Saints have worked on getting the ball out faster, and it's improved over the weeks. But pass protection remains a major concern, and with a switch at center, right guard, and right tackle, the Saints could be in for a long day.

I don't think I even have to elaborate here. The Saints are a bottom-five rushing offense in the league, and are coming off a game where running backs averaged 1.5 yards per carry against one of the league's worst run defenses in the league. Now New Orleans will be facing a team that is top-five against the run.

Getting the run started has been a problem all season, and really, it's more of a Kellen Moore issue than it is a running backs issue. But if the Saints are going to have any chance at keeping up with Chicago's high-powered offense, it will be by controlling the game on the ground.

The Saints have two of the most dangerous pass rushers this season, with Carl Granderson logging 8.5 sacks and Chase Young getting 8 of his own. Fortunately for the Saints, they'll be facing a quarterback who holds on to the ball the longest in the NFL. Unfortunately for New Orleans, Caleb Williams' time from getting the ball and releasing it hasn't translated into sacks.

Over the last two games, Williams has been sacked just twice and has become a playmaker in the face of the pass rush. The former first overall pick's stats haven't looked great this season, throwing just 8 touchdowns to 5 interceptions, but he has grown into a leader and can command an offense with the best of them. If the Saints want a chance at winning this game, they'll likely have to take down the star quarterback three or more times and create impossible goal-to-go situations.

#2868. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:
