#2867. Lakers squeak by lowly Kings to get back on track

The Lakers continue to look like a team in need of the benefits that are supposed to come from this stretch in Southern California.

Because after looking like one of the NBA's best teams for 3 ½ weeks regardless of the competition, they've struggled against some of the league's bottom-feeders during their four-game homestand.

Luka Dončić shoots a 3-point basket against the Sacramento Kings. NBAE via Getty Images

Cameron Carr drives to the basket. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers continue to look like a team in need of the benefits that are supposed to come from this stretch in Southern California. NBAE via Getty Images

#2865. Previewing the New York Jets’ opposition: Los Angeles Chargers

The last time the Jets beat the Chargers, they were still representing San Diego and Plaxico Burress scored three touchdowns. Since that time, New York have lost five straight times to them, including a 27-6 drubbing in their most recent meeting in 2023.

Let's break down the Chargers' roster going position by position:

Justin Herbert was a pro bowler last season, but he's having a rough year in 2026, with career-worst numbers in almost every category. In his first six seasons, he averaged 27 touchdown passes and 11 interceptions per year. However, over halfway through the 2026 season, he has thrown for just six touchdown passes and already has 11 interceptions.

He didn't have a very productive game as a passer against the Ravens on Monday night, but at least he managed not to throw an interception for the first time all year as the Chargers finally won their first game to improve to 1-8. He also rushed for a season-high 51 yards and a touchdown.

DJ Uiagalelei is the backup but he's completed just two NFL passes in his career.

Incredibly, five of the Chargers' eight active offensive linemen are rookies, although this is hardly by design. They have five offensive linemen on injured reserve, including well known names like Rashawn Slater, Tyler Biadasz, Trevor Penning and Trey Pipkins. Pipkins was activated for practice last week, though, so his return is possible on Sunday. His official game status is questionable.

Unsurprisingly, this has led to the Chargers struggling to protect Herbert, who has been sacked 33 times. Only Deshaun Watson has been sacked more often that that this season.

The only two players to start every game so far have been second round rookie Jake Slaughter at center and veteran right guard Cole Strange.

At the moment, Joe Alt is at left tackle, with sixth round rookie Logan Taylor at left guard. Slater and Penning had started off the year on the left side, but Alt moved across from right tackle and Taylor stepped in when each of them got hurt. Since Alt's move, fourth round rookie Travis Burke has been starting at right tackle.

Alt has a team-high seven penalties and Strange, who has also given up a team-high three sacks, has six.

The three players currently on the bench are veteran guard Kayode Awosika, sixth round tackle Alex Harkey and the undrafted center Jacob Spomer. Awosika and Harkey have started two games each this season.

Los Angeles' top running back, Omarion Hampton, last season's number one pick, is still averaging less than four yards per carry behind the banged-up line, although the Chargers rank 10th in the NFL for rushing offense. He's coming off his first 100-yard rushing performance of the season in the win over the Ravens.

Keaton Mitchell and Kimani Vidal both get work as backups to Hampton but they combined for just four yards on five carries in the Ravens win. They have caught 30 passes and scored three touchdowns between them, though.

Scott Matlock is also on the roster as a fullback with Alec Ingold on injured reserve.

The leading receiver so far this year for the Chargers is last year's second round pick Tre Harris who has 26 catches for 348 yards but no touchdowns.

You would have expected Ladd McConkey and Quentin Johnson to produce more alongside Harris but they've only started eight games between them and combined for just 31 catches. However, they've flashed big play potential with Johnson averaging 19 yards per catch and McConkey averaging 17. On Monday, McConkey – who is questionable for Sunday – was inactive and Harris was the only Charger with more than 16 receiving yards.

At tight end, Oronde Gadsen II has caught 20 passes and Charlie Kolar has caught 15, but David Njoku has been a non-factor. Njoku could play more this week, though, because Kolar has been dealing with a concussion and is listed as questionable.

The backups at wide receiver are Theo Wease Jr., KeAndre Lambert-Smith, Derius Davis and fourth round rookie Brenen Thompson. They've combined for 12 catches, half of which were by Lambert-Smith. Thompson is listed as doubtful for Sunday.

The Chargers operate with a 3-4 system that employs Jamaree Caldwell, Dalvin Tomlinson and Teair Tart as starters. They are poor against the pass, but middle of the pack against the run, and held the Ravens to 16 yards on 15 carries on Monday night.

Fifth round rookie Nick Barrett and Justin Eboigbe are the backups inside. All five have played at least 140 snaps with Tomlinson having contributed 2.5 sacks.

The Chargers drafted Akheem Mesidor in the first round but he's currently coming off the bench behind veteran Khalil Mack and 2025 pro bowler Tuli Tuipolotu. While none of the three has more than three sacks, they've combined for 8.5. Mack leads the team with six tackles for loss, while Tuipolotu leads them with 16 quarterback hits.

At inside linebacker, Daiyan Henley plays a full-time role and wears the headset. He leads the team with 62 tackles but has been bothered by a knee issue all year, which even nixed a potential trade to Denver. The knee issue has ruled him out for Sunday.

The other starter has been former Jet Del'Shawn Phillips, although Henley was sometimes the only off-ball linebacker on the field. Backups Troy Dye and Marlowe Wax don't get to play much but Wax should have a bigger role with Henley out.

Bud Dupree and Caleb Murphy are the backups on the outside but neither has played much.

Aside from all their injuries on the offensive line, the Chargers have also been deeply impacted by pro bowl safety Derwin James being on injured reserve.

Tony Jefferson and Elijah Molden are the current starters at safety, although RJ Mickens and fourth round rookie Genesis Smith have also played over 200 snaps each.

Kendall Williamson is also on the roster but hasn't played on defense and has been listed as out anyway.

Tarheeb Still and Donte Jackson are the starting cornerbacks with Still reverting to the slot in nickel packages and Cam Hart entering the game on the outside. Hart has two interceptions and a team-high eight pass breakups.

Still practiced all week but showed up on the injury report on Friday and is listed as questionable.

Backups Rodney Shelley, Isas Waxter and Deane Leonard have all played less than 100 snaps at cornerback. Shelley is another undrafted rookie but he's been ruled out, while Waxter is also listed as questionable.

The Chargers' kicking game is pretty solid with kicker Cameron Dicker having only missed two kicks all season. He also has the third-lowest opponents' kickoff return average in the NFL. While punter JK Scott's raw numbers are not eye-popping, Pro Football Focus currently ranks him first in the league based on their grades.

Davis, Mitchell and Vidal have all seen work on kickoff returns, with nobody managing a 35-yard return so far this year. The Jets should be wary of Davis, though, as he had an 87-yard punt return for a touchdown against them in 2023. He's averaging 10 yards per return on punts with Thompson and McConkey listed as his backups.

Jets fans may recall what a good special teams contributor Phillips was, and he's continued in that vein by leading the Chargers in special teams tackles again despite moving into the starting unit. He led the entire NFL last year.

Wax, Murphy and Dye are also productive, while the primary gunners have been Lambert-Smith and the backup cornerbacks.

#2864. Warren Buffett quadruples Thanksgiving gifts: A breakdown of how much wealth he's giving away

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Warren Buffett, 96, more than quadrupled his Thanksgiving donations to four family foundations this year to $5.5 billion.

The gifts consist of Berkshire Class B shares that have been converted from his roughly $160 billion of Class A shares.

The increase is in line with his new goal, announced last July, to give away all of his Berkshire Hathaway shares "within about eight years," with no additional annual donations going to the Gates Foundation.

Around $2.2 billion of shares goes to the Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, named for his late first wife, with the three foundations run by his children, Susie Buffett's Sherwood Foundation, the Howard G. Buffett Foundation, and Peter Buffett's NoVo Foundation each getting $1.1 billion each.

In July of last year, Buffett gave shares worth $4.6 billion to the Gates Foundation with another $1.4 billion going to the family foundations.

This July, the family foundations received $5.9 billion in stock, bringing the year's total to $11.4 billion, an increase of almost 55% from last year, even as the Gates Foundation received nothing.

In comments included in the Berkshire news release this week announcing the new gifts, Buffett reaffirmed his confidence in his children's ability to disburse his fortune over time, saying they "have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity."

He also said his confidence in Berkshire is "as strong as ever."

Recalling that in last year's lengthier Thanksgiving message he had said, "Kindness is costless but also priceless," Buffett wrote, " A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie [Munger] and I ever bought."

"Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received.

"Much of my good fortune has come in the form of people who were kind to me and expected nothing in return. I'm grateful to every one of them.

"This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

Berkshire Hathaway's fourth quarter rally that had produced a string of record highs cooled this week as both classes of its stock posted five straight daily declines.

Last Friday, Berkshire was up 19.1% since October 1. Now that gain has been cut to 14.5%

The S&P's decline of 8.6% this week, however, was even bigger than Berkshire's 5-day drop, as investors sold to lock in profits from AI-related stocks.

The benchmark index, along with the Dow Industrials, are in a correction after falling 10% from their recent highs, and the Nasdaq Composite has erased all of its year-to-date gains.

Berkshire's smaller decline increased its year-to-date outperformance of the S&P to 12.5 percentage points.

One Wall Street firm, however, thinks Berkshire's run-up has run its course.

Argus Research downgraded the B shares to hold from buy Friday, saying the stock is "fully valued" at about 25 times next year's estimate for operating earnings per share.

As foreshadowed by its 10-Q financial report released almost two weeks ago, Berkshire Hathaway's Q3 13F portfolio snapshot filed with the SEC on Monday showed it wasn't doing much buying and selling of equities this summer.

And the two largest additions, Lennar and Bending Spoons, had been previously disclosed, along with additional purchases at the start of the fourth quarter.

The most notable change we didn't expect was a 12% increase in Berkshire's Delta Air Lines position, bringing it just short of a 10% stake, now valued at $5.2 billion.

Exceeding 10% triggers a number of regulatory burdens, including the requirement to promptly disclose new buying or selling, so we may not see additional purchases going forward.

Delta's return to the portfolio after Warren Buffett sold all of Berkshire's airline stocks in 2020 has been something of a surprise given his bumpy ride with the industry over the years.

As expected, Berkshire continued to trim its Bank of America stake. After nine consecutive quarters of sales, it's been cut by 56% but remains the fifth largest portfolio holding at $21.4 billion.

Berkshire also cut another financial, Capital One, by almost 62%, although it was already a relatively small position, now worth $207 million.

Also notable are two positions that didn't change.

After adding $17 billion in Q2 to the Alphabet bet that had only been initiated last summer, Berkshire defied some expectations by keeping the holding unchanged.

Its 106 million shares are valued at $28.1 billion.

And for the fourth consecutive quarter, Berkshire's largest equity holding, Apple, didn't change. With a big boost from the stock's year-to-date gain of 26.1%, the position is now valued at more than $78 billion.

Warren Buffett loves to eat what many would call junk food and drink a lot of sugary beverages. A quarter-century ago, when he was 70, Buffett explained why he still thought a life insurance company would rate his life expectancy "considerably better than the average."

AUDIENCE MEMBER: Every time I hear what you like to eat, Warren, it makes me wonder what your cholesterol level is — (laughter) — or if you even worry about it...

WARREN BUFFETT: I do know the number, and I don't remember it. My doctor tells me, "It's a little high," but if he says it's a little high, it means it isn't that high, or he would — because he always tries to push me into making a few changes in my life.

But he — I've got a wonderful doctor. And I was lucky last year, because I hadn't been in to see him for about five years. And — (laughter) — due to — those guys cost a lot of money, I mean. (Laughter)

And due to purely an accident, a reaction to some other medicine I was given when I was out of the city, he got a hold of me, and then he shamed me into having a physical. And it was extremely lucky, because I had a polyp in the colon that would have probably caused trouble, you know, within a couple of years.

I would say that if you ask my doctor, he would want me to make a few changes, but he would also say that my life expectancy is probably a lot better than the average person of 70.

You know, I have no stress whatsoever. Zero. You know, I mean, I get to do what I love to do every day. You know, and I'm surrounded by people that are terrific.

So that problem in life just doesn't exist for me. You know, and I don't smoke or drink or, well, we'll end it right there. (Laughter)

And so, you know, if you were an underwriter for a life company, you would rate me considerably better than the average. You'd rate Charlie better than average, too.

And I'm sure that, you know, I could change it slightly, perhaps, on the probabilities, you know, if I change my diet dramatically or something. But it's very unlikely to happen.

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,210,976,168,580

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

#2862. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2861. Game Recap: Suns survive late Jazz rally to secure 111-101 NBA Cup victory

Man, this game was an emotional rollercoaster, especially late, but the Suns pulled this one out in a big way. For a team that started 1-5, they have turned things around quickly, winning three straight and only losing two in their last nine games. This is a good trend for a team still figuring things out on the fly with their starting lineup. It's another big win, as this was an NBA Emirates Cup game, which helped them bounce back after their first loss in Group A to the Denver Nuggets.

This game started hot for Phoenix, who took a commanding lead in the first half and held on to it through the third as a lot of free throws were made. In the fourth, the Jazz tried to climb back, cutting the deficit to four, but it was too little, too late as the Suns took care of business on the road. Even though the Suns allowed 17 OREB, their 19 points on 15 turnovers helped them early and led to this deserved win.

Devin Booker led Phoenix with 23 points, two rebounds, and three assists, while Miles Bridges and Dillon Brooks each had 18. Bench mob beasts Collin Gillespie and Jordan Goodwin sealed the win: Gillespie added 16 points, 4 rebounds, and 5 assists, while Goodwin grabbed 9 rebounds and 4 assists, doing much of the dirty work alongside Bridges.

For the Jazz, Darryn Peterson had 20 points, ten rebounds, and eight assists, which was close to a triple-double, while Jaxson Hayes caused havoc on the glass. He finished with 18 points and 14 rebounds, a big reason Utah stayed in this one. Brice Sensabaugh also got hot late, adding 18 of his own points with five threes to keep the Jazz thriving from behind the arc.

For the Phoenix Suns, coming out of the gate hot was needed to get to a three-game win streak. For the Suns, though, before they could score a basket, they had to win their first challenge call that night because of the refs. The Suns starters did look solid in their first stint, as they got Dillon Brooks going early, with Keyonte George guarding him for an easy four points. Phoenix got off to an early run, but the Jazz matched it and tied it up at 12.

Sadly for the Suns, they would have to use their second challenge just seven minutes into the game when Booker was hit across the face by a driving Ace Bailey. Even though Phoenix won both challenges, it was tough to lose them with 40 minutes of game time left. This allowed the bench to come in and resume where they left off. Jordan Goodwin and Collin Gillespie made it known when they got out there, with Gillespie making the first three of the night for both teams.

With Khaman Maluach and Ryan Dunn checking in, the Suns seemed more aggressive on the boards and limited the Jazz's second-chance points. The Suns gave up 12 OREB in the first half, and that cannot continue into the second half. With the Jazz finding some threes late, they brought the game closer to the end of the first quarter, but Booker made the last shot count, hitting a buzzer-beater off a full-court pass from Miles Bridges. The Suns led 26-23 at the end of the first quarter.

Heading into the second, we saw the bench continue to prove they are the best in the league, with Gillespie making some tough buckets and Luke Kennard getting an easy five points on two quick shots. Unfortunately, the trouble on the glass is an issue, as Jaxson Hayes is dominating and getting multiple tip-ins and one-and-one opportunities.

Though this was the case, Brooks and Bridges provided the Suns with the scoring to take their largest lead so far, 43-30, with a strong run and Bridges getting comfortable. Sadly, the Suns saw an injury when Ryan Dunn caught a knee to the thigh and went to the locker room. He provided solid defense and rebounding, allowing the offense to flow better with ball movement.

The Jazz, though, didn't want to go out quiet as Darryn Peterson started showing off his skills on both ends. Bailey secured a tip-in at the end, but the Suns saw some nice moves from Malauch in his second stint. The big caught a nice block and made some easy passes for putbacks and points. The Suns finished the first half up 61-49. Brooks and Gillepsie lead the way for the Suns with eleven points each, while Bridges. Booker and Maluach have eight. It's truly been a team game offensively, with the ball being shared, especially in the second unit. For the Jazz, Peterson has 12 points, while Hayes has 11, and Sensabaugh has 3 made threes. To win this game, the Suns have to win the rebounding battle and control the possession.

To kick off the second half, the Suns found out that Ryan Dunn would not return, leaving his minutes in limbo. That is where Koa Peat got some burn and, unfortunately, didn't get too much of it, as he picked up some early fouls. The Suns did a better job on the glass, limiting the Jazz's second-chance points, but the Jazz were still getting to the charity stripe.

This is where the game took a toll on me, as it dragged on with both teams finding opportunities to score only at the free-throw line. Brooks and Bridges, though, continued to show their offensive flow, with Bridges driving and delivering some nice paint scoring alongside some boards. The Suns got the lead back to 12, and Booker found a rhythm of his own, scoring 10 points in the quarter to bring his total to 18.

The thirteen Jazz turnovers really cost them in this game, and with the Suns doing a better job of limiting them on the glass, the Suns looked on track for an easy win if they closed out the fourth. Although this is the Phoenix Suns we are talking about, they always make your heart skip a beat, even when they play the worst teams.

The fourth quarter opened with Brice Sensabaugh getting red hot and quickly scoring four points. The Suns answered with tough shots of their own from Gillespie, but the Jazz weren't bowing out of this one. With more rim pressure from Hayes, the Jazz got back on the glass and caused some disruptions. Peterson also showed off his quickness to get to the cup and now had a double-double with some time left.

Bridges once again helped this team, alongside Booker, keep them afloat with some nice baskets. Brooks picked up his fifth, then eventually his sixth foul, and was fouled out of the game, which was not a good look as the Jazz rallied back. With more made threes, the Suns, who had a 16-point lead at one point, were down to just four with under a minute left.

Luckily for this team, Bridges was on tonight and made multiple nice inbound passes to Booker to get a guaranteed bucket, and here was another example of that. Booker then went on to get another three points by hunting his shots, and the Suns pulled off a 111-101 win. This is now the team's longest win streak this season, at three, and they just secured their first NBA Cup win this year. Overall, it wasn't the best win on paper, but it was solid nonetheless.

The Suns continue their road trip, heading to New Orleans to take on the Pelicans.

#2858. Tech outages are fueling a new doomsday economy

Americans are shopping like there's no tomorrow.

A spate of high-profile technology outages that grounded planes, halted email access and slowed bank transactions have left many people worried about artificial intelligence-driven cyberattacks and the state of the U.S. energy grid.

Government officials have dismissed rumors and speculation circulating online that there could be a nefarious culprit for the series of malfunctions. Despite those assurances, consumers like Sabrina Cepeda are readying for a larger-scale technology blackout or other emergency event.

In recent days, the Houston resident has purchased propane and lighter fluid for cooking and heating equipment that can be used without electricity. The mother of three also replenished her backup stockpiles of non-perishable foods, hygiene products and allergy medicine in the event that her family is homebound for several days.

"I have a way to keep my family fed, clean, hydrated, warm and safe," said Cepeda, 39. "You want to have these things and not need them, rather than need them and not have them."

The doomsday economy is catching a bid. Wall Street and retailers are taking note.

At least half a dozen major technology outages took place in the U.S. between Nov. 12 and Nov. 17. In that time frame, spending at brands specializing in emergency products spiked about 400% compared with the same period a year prior, according to card data analyzed exclusively for CNBC by Consumer Edge.

At Ready America, which sells products like emergency survival kits, prepackaged water and high-calorie food bars, sales through e-commerce platforms have seen a roughly five-fold increase in recent days. In addition, corporate customers who keep the Vista, California-based company's supplies in their offices in case of emergency have called in to order more.

The company has rarely seen this much demand since the Covid pandemic sent Americans into lockdown more than six years ago, said Ready America President Trevyn Reese. As a result, Ready America asked its suppliers to scale up production. The family-owned business has faced a shortage of water purification tablets, and redirected shoppers to alternative offerings like prepackaged water pouches.

"With everything going on in the news right now, we've seen a large spike of people wanting to get prepared," said Reese.

While the string of outages brought power-related concerns to a boiling point, data shows that worries had been bubbling under the surface for weeks.

Shoppers began significantly ramping up buying from emergency preparedness brands late last month, according to Consumer Edge. Around that time, Fox News reported that the Pentagon was preparing U.S. military bases to operate for at least 90 days without external support. Exacerbating those fears was the ongoing war with Iran that President Donald Trump signaled could ramp back up.

Firearm trade group NSSF said that gun sales rose about 8% in October from the same month a year prior. Retailers and gun manufacturers have anecdotally reported that demand has remained elevated into November, which NSSF said can be attributed to a combination of uncertainty tied to the midterm election and the recent outages.

"If people are investing in firearms and ammunition, they're telling you very directly, with their wallet, what their priority is," said Mark Oliva, NSSF's managing director of public affairs. "You're literally investing in a product that's going to be able to protect your life and protect the lives of your family."

Now, the doomsday talk has begun making its way to Wall Street.

Mizuho told clients on Monday that consumer worries about the outages — coupled with what the bank called "apocalyptic concerns" swirling on social media — drove shoppers to stock up on emergency supplies at Costco. As a result, Mizuho said it expects a key measure of non-fuel sales growth for Costco in November to rise above 10% for the first time since 2022.

And RBC said so-called doomsday prepping has had a "real impact" on buying behavior around the globe over recent weeks. Still, the Canadian bank reported that the trend has so far boosted sales for only a small basket of items, such as shelf-stable proteins, water purification products and dry-pantry foods.

Clients of Richard McWhorter, a private wealth advisor at SRM, have been rushing to Costco to buy goods in bulk and splurging for power generators. McWhorter said he's also fielded questions about how to prepare for a situation in which the financial system goes down.

"I've never seen anything like this in my life," McWhorter said. "There's the financial side of what I do, and then there's the psychological side. And the past two weeks have been much more psychological."

Metal dealer American Hartford Gold reported an uptick in customer inquiries this week. Company president Max Baecker said he heard from some customers that their interest in products like gold — long considered a safe-haven asset during times of turmoil — was driven by newfound concern about being overly reliant on digital financial networks following a glitch at JPMorgan Chase that affected customer transactions for several hours.

Vice President JD Vance abruptly canceled his appearance at a New York Times summit on Wednesday to attend meetings about the technology outages. A day prior, Jay Clayton, the U.S. director of national intelligence, told CNBC that the recent outages were not a cyberattack or a coordinated effort by a foreign nation.

Still, Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., told CNBC on Wednesday that people were "freaked out" by the outages. Warner, who acts as the vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, said that the U.S. was "lucky" to have not yet seen a major AI-generated cyberattack or crisis due to AI agents going rogue.

Investors may begin pushing companies to accelerate their cybersecurity spending out of an abundance of caution around rogue AI agents following the outages, according to Macro Hive CEO Bilal Hafeez. Such a situation would be reminiscent of the antivirus software push seen during the dot-com boom, he said.

The Global X Cybersecurity ETF (BUG) has climbed around 3% over the past two weeks, bucking the downtrend seen among AI stocks and the broader technology trade. The Nasdaq Composite on Friday fell into negative territory for 2026, putting the tech-heavy index on track for its first losing year since 2022.

To be sure, not everyone on social media has bought into the idea that they should be prepping for a doomsday scenario. In satirical videos posted on TikTok, users joked that they were gearing up by placing orders in the Starbucks drive-thru or purchasing significant quantities of alcohol.

Still, the topic appears to be on the minds of a growing number of Americans. U.S. Google searches for the term "prepping" have more than doubled when comparing the past month with the same period a year prior. On YouTube, queries for videos about "survivalism," a term describing the practice of preparing for emergencies, have soared by more than 5,000% over the same time frame.

Cepeda, the Houston mom, sees emergency prep efforts as a way to be safe rather than sorry. She's previously tapped into backup food stashes during extreme weather events like Hurricane Harvey in 2017 and Winter Storm Uri in 2021.

But this year is the first time that she's felt she needed to be prepared for a potential emergency caused by technology outages, she said. Cepeda and her husband have ensured they have enough cash on hand and fuel in their cars if there's a broader hit to the U.S. power grid, she said.

"We rely so much on technology," Cepeda said. "You need to be able to do these things the more traditional, old-fashioned way."

#2857. Utah Jazz vs Phoenix Suns: Player Grades

By my money, this was the most disappointing loss of the year thus far. Riding high after two road wins, the team looked exhilarated for the first time all year and the home fans were excited to finally have something to latch onto. You could feel how badly the crowd wanted to root hard for their team, but instead, their team simply decided to not show up. The Jazz only lost by 10 points tonight, but if you could only analyze the game based off of body language and effort, you would guess that it was more than that. They didn't hustle after loose balls, they made no attempts to patch their poor decision making, and they looked defeated the whole night. This was a bad loss, and the grades will represent that – not a lot of positives you can take from this one.

Note: These grades are more-or-less relative to the player. An A grade for Darryn Peterson is different than an A grade for Tony Bradley.

It is a make or miss league, and Ace Bailey missed. Honestly, that isn't too surprising; it's been the status quo up to this point in the season. One of the most underrated skills in the league, fascinatingly enough, is actually making shots – a reputation alone doesn't help quite as much. To this point, Ace has a limit on his ceiling until his jump shots go in at a consistent rate, which has not been the case in his collegiate or NBA career. He will rarely go 0-7, but a better shooter will have nights like this even less frequently. Additionally, his defense has become less and less impressive as the year has progressed; tonight, he wasn't nearly as loud as he was at the beginning of the year. Without event creation on defense, self creation on offense, or the ability to hit open threes, Ace really isn't providing anything. Will Hardy recognized this, benching Ace for the closing stretch. This team's potential is very tightly knit to Ace's, and games like this just won't cut it.

After heaps of well-deserved praise after his recent stretch, Mo Bamba had a major regression-t0-the-mean game tonight. He was essentially invisible; the Suns were attacking him relentlessly and he was unable to use his size to make them pay. He looked comfortable taking threes…perhaps too comfortable; I think I prefer when he takes them with defenders sagging off completely, instead of in the face of solid closeouts. Ultimately, this harsh grade was given in the light of his recent games. These were the minutes I expected we'd get from Mo when the Jazz picked him up initially, but because he's shown me more, I've come to expect more. So, while I've loved the Bamba experience thus far, this game, hopefully, acted as a return to reality for those already championing Bamba as the Jazz's answer at the center position.

This was one of the worst offensive games that I can remember from Keyonte. The missed shots were one thing, but the lack of effort and body language were really discouraging. Keyonte has taken on the mantle of leader, but the responsibilities of that title are not relinquished during games where his threes are not going in. It's fun to be a leader when you beat the Lakers in L.A., but it's essential to be a leader when you are battling the Suns in a slog two nights later, and you are simply not doing that when you nonchalantly let opponents steal offensive rebounds from under your nose. I restrained myself from delivering an F due to some genuinely solid defensive contests, but I am placing the majority of this game's ugliness squarely on Keyonte's shoulders.

Josh Green is Josh Green. He can't dribble or pass, which is detrimental in lineups where he is playing alongside 2-3 other players who also can't dribble or pass. But he'll hit an open corner three or two, and he is easily our best defensive option against any star scorer, ranging from 6'4 to 6'8. Devin Booker ended with 23 points, but he wasn't particularly efficient, partially because Green understood that the reason he gets minutes is to make life difficult for the bucket-getter that he's lined up against. The final ATO was his worst coverage of the night, but that did not define his night – we did not lose this game because of Josh Green losing Devin Booker at the end of the game.

Very impressive night from Jaxson Hayes, where he reminded the fanbase that, until this season, he was a far more established player than Mo Bamba for a reason. One underrated skill of his is his timing on his rolls to the rim – he plays it at just the right pace where the ball-handler is able to hit him right in the most sensitive pocket of the defense, and tonight the Suns didn't really have an answer besides fouling. The missed free throws were frustrating, and I don't feel like Hayes ever plays as a 7 footer on defense (more of a shot blocker than a rim protector, and tonight he wasn't even blocking shots), but providing a consistent and efficient offensive outlet is something that shouldn't be undersold.

People aren't too quick to offer Svi praise these days, but he provided one of the better combinations of shooting and effort on the team tonight. I get it, I get it, his turnovers are frustrating, especially for a player that really shouldn't be dribbling, but tonight he understood that someone had to make something happen, which helps me give him a bit of a pass for his 3 turnovers tonight. At the end of the day, if Svi can make jump shots, he's a solid role player – let's hope that his recent efficiency continues on throughout the season.

Maybe I should've spent more attention on what Okogie was doing on defense, but it didn't pop off the screen tonight like it sometimes does. Okogie is somehow an even more simplistic player than Green – if he doesn't have a small, not-too-quick guard to pester, and the shot's not dropping, he really isn't providing too much to the team. He doesn't take much away – he'll shoot when open, and bring intensity at the very least – but he didn't contribute much of anything to the team tonight.

Interesting game for Peterson. He went 0-7 from three, which could make you think that I'd penalize him the same way I did Ace and Keyonte, but Peterson was able to get his fingerprints all over the game tonight in a way that his other prestigious youngster teammates could not. Some of his passes tonight were legitimately inspiring, and gave me hope that his long-term position might actually be point guard – his one hand dart to Green in the corner after a shifty drive was particularly impressive. Additionally, even though he struggled when matched up with a shifty point guard in Gillespie, I still think he gave some serious defensive flashes, at the very least on the defensive glass. Ultimately, I think much of a player's value is based on what they can provide to a team when their shot isn't falling, and tonight, Peterson provided a bit of everything while Ace and Keyonte did not. Some nights will be off nights, but that can be overcome with skills that are not dependent on the variance inherent to jump shots – Peterson seems to already have a strong understanding of that fact.

The defense wasn't awesome, and the decision making wasn't always great, but when he is making his shots, Brice provides so much value to this team because we simply do not have a surplus of players who can do what he does. He is a versatile shot-maker who can succeed even when faced with contests, and that cannot be said for a lot of the players competing for his minutes. Until we get some other player who can fill this niche on the Jazz bench, I can't get behind the prevailing sentiment, which is to call for his immediate dismissal from the consistently active Jazzmen. Brice brings an NBA skill that can completely alter a team's fortunes, which he did tonight – without his efficient shooting, I think the Jazz would've been dead in the water.

Who stood out for you? Which grades would you change? Sound off below!

#2855. Miami Dolphins offensive line review: Tracking the guards in Week 10

When you lose a close game to a bad opponent and your quarterback gets strip-sacked for a defensive touchdown, you might assume the offensive line had a terrible afternoon. The reality is more complicated. The Dolphins nearly completed a 14-point comeback but ultimately fell 24-22 to the Colts in Week 10, a game that highlighted a seemingly downward trend for the team as a whole.

That may be especially true for the offensive line, where rookie left guard Kadyn Proctor and second-year right guard Jonah Savaiinaea remain key pieces of the Dolphins' future. While the offense struggled as a whole, player development doesn't stop because of one ugly afternoon, and that's especially true for two young guards expected to be part of the foundation moving forward. Each week, we take a closer look at Proctor and Savaiinaea and examine what analysts are saying about their most recent performances.

When you think of the ideal offensive line, you're probably thinking of five guys who have played together for a long time and who understand what the quarterback likes to do in certain game situations. At this moment in time, the Dolphins have none of that. In Week 5, Savaiinaea misses most of the game due to injury. Proctor misses Week 8 while in concussion protocol. Against the Colts, Andrew Meyer played the entire game at center while Aaron Brewer is treated for his own concussion, and the team is led by a brand new quarterback in Kyle McCord. With all of these injuries and new faces, you can't really be surprised when plays break down and players don't know where to go.

A big reason why the offensive line held up as well as it did – even without Brewer – is Kadyn Proctor. There's an old line about offensive linemen: Strong o-line performance slips by the average viewer – it's when you make a mistake that you stand out. Proctor has been steady most of the season, but especially lately: Fewer bad reps as the season moves along, with steady, consistent growth. His PFF grades averages out to a slightly above league-average 61.9, which is remarkable for a rookie, and his pass protection over the past two games is bordering on elite. LockedOn Dolphins' Kyle Crabbs said this week that Proctor's last three games "have graded as the three best games that he has played this season."

As for Jonah Savaiinaea, the picture is a little murkier. Crabbs has been increasingly critical of his play since the bye week, arguing in the same video that the Colts game featured several examples of the inconsistency that plagued him as a rookie. One missed block late in the game potentially cost Ollie Gordon a significant gain during Miami's final drive. That said, PFF ranked his performance against the Colts as his second-best overall and against the pass. Maybe Savaiinaea is just slow to develop (remember how long it took Austin Jackson to turn into a serviceable starter?), and the last month has raised questions about how much of Savaiinaea's hot start was sustainable. Jonah for sure has more development to do, especially against the pass (PFF grades <45 in 3 of the past 4 games). I still have hope.

One last point we touched on before: Perhaps more than anywhere else on the team, offensive line performance is a team effort, especially as opposing defenses become more nuanced and creative. Offensive-line play is often evaluated as if the five linemen operate in isolation. They don't. A failed run can be traced to a guard losing a block, but it can also be caused by a tight end missing an assignment (cough Dulcich cough), a receiver failing to seal the edge, a protection call that never gets communicated or the absence of an All-Pro center who normally keeps everything organized. Against Indianapolis, we saw examples of all of the above.

That's why a strip-sack, a stuffed run or a pressure allowed doesn't always tell the full story. When looking at o-line scoring, I try to remember that context matters.

The tracker will be updated each week throughout the season. For a more in-depth breakdown of Proctor's and Savaiinaea's performance, check out offensive line Coach Eric Smith's film analysis of the game, which you can find on his YouTube channel, Focused on Fins.

#2854. New Mexico men, Alabama women capture NCAA cross country titles

TERRE HAUTE, Ind. (AP) — The New Mexico men capped an unbeaten season and the Alabama women upset pre-race BYU at the NCAA Division I cross country championships on Saturday.

NC State's Hannah Gapes won the women's individual title, covering the 6,000-meter course in 18-minutes, 44.3 seconds, lowering her own course record. Louisville's Elsingi Kipruot won the men's 10K in a course-record time of 28:27.7, pulling away in the final two kilometers.

New Mexico placed three runners in the top 10 — Pasanjo Kitonga 7th, Collins Kiprotich 8th and Vincent Chirchir 10th — and scored 69 points. Colorado was second with 107 points, two ahead of Alabama.

It was the first NCAA men's team title for New Mexico, although the Lobos' Habtom Samuel won the individual crown last year.

In becoming Louisville's first individual champion, Kipruto easily broke the course record at the LaVern Gibson Championship Course. Liberty's Sam Chelanga held the record at 28:41.2 in the 2009 championship.

Dismus Lokira of Alabama was second, three seconds back with Denis Kipngetich another two seconds behind in third.

Alabama women also won a first title, led by Caren Kiplagat in third and Cynthia Jemutai in 10th. Doris Lemngole won the previous two individual titles for the Crimson Tide.

Alabama tallied 120 points with West Virginia second at 145 and top-seeded BYU at 152.

Gapes' time broke her course record of 18:57.6 she set at Pre-Nationals in October. She is the first native of New Zealand to win the title in 22 years and fifth NC State woman to win the title.

Salma Elbadra of South Carolina was second in 18:51.8.

#2853. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

Center Moritz Wagner reacts during the second half of the Nets' 113-111 loss to the Magic on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

Orlando forward Franz Wagner (22) celebrates during the Magic's win over the Nets on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

#2850. Broncos vs. Raiders: 3 Players to Watch in Week 11

This Sunday, the Denver Broncos take on the Las Vegas Raiders in a classic AFC West rivalry matchup. With an extra week of preparation, the Broncos had plenty of time to figure out a winning gameplan. In order to earn their eighth win of the season, these players need to execute at a high level.

All eyes will be on Bo Nix this Sunday. Nix hasn't experienced the uptick in production Broncos Country expected in his third season. Through nine games, he is completing just 61-percent of his passes [a career low], thrown for just 1,730 yards, 11 touchdowns and 4 interceptions. Coming off an ankle injury, he hasn't been as productive as a runner, though has added 116 yards and two touchdowns on the ground.

Over the past three games, the former first-round pick has played better than he started the season. In that timeframe, he has six total touchdowns and only one turnover. Things appear to be going in the right direction, and I remain optimistic he can turn it up in the second half of the season.

On Sunday, Nix will face Raiders' defense that is allowing nearly 250 passing yards a game—a bottom 10 mark in the NFL. With that in mind, I'm hopeful to see more production from the third-year signal caller. Nix has just one game this year over 250 yards passing—his Week 2 effort versus the Jaguars.

With extra time to gameplan, I am confident he and the offense will be able to take advantage and put together one of their best performances of the season. I think Nix sizzles in his first game after the bye and starts the second half of the season on a high note.

Earlier in the week, the Broncos rewarded cornerback Riley Moss with a four-year contract extension worth $67 million dollars. The fourth-year pro has been a key starter in Vance Joseph's defense the past two and a half seasons.

It's no easy task playing opposite of All-Pro Patrick Surtain II. For the most part, Moss has passed that assignment with flying colors. The former Iowa standout has a knack for defending passes but has just three interceptions in his career. I'd like to see him start taking the ball away more.

Sunday's matchup provides that opportunity. The Raiders will be starting Fernando Mendoza. Last weekend against the Seahawks, he struggled but was relatively safe with the football. I'm hopeful Denver's defensive front seven can get after Mendoza early and often and force him into some bad decisions.

I don't expect the 2026 NFL Draft's top pick to test Surtain much at all. That means Moss should be in store for a high-volume day target-wise. Can he rise to the occasion and make a big splash play in his first game after his extension? We are fixing to find out.

When the Broncos traded for Jaylen Waddle this offseason, fans across Broncos Country were confident he would propel their passing attack to the next level. Count me in as one of them. Unfortunately, Waddle hasn't had that type of impact yet.

His Week 2 performance against the Jacksonville Jaguars sticks out. However, he hasn't had over 50 receiving yards in any other contest thus far. Through nine games, the former 2021 first-round pick is sporting career lows across the board. In order for Denver's offense to take flight in the second half of the season, Waddle has to perform much better.

The Raiders have been opportunistic on defense creating turnovers. That's what they're good at. Otherwise, they sport a bottom 10 pass defense. Las Vegas has given up the most passing touchdowns of any defense with 22 on the season. They are also allowing the most yards per reception of any team in the NFL.

Opposing quarterbacks have the best rating in the NFL going up against their secondary. With that in mind, this Sunday seems like a prime matchup for Waddle (and the aforementioned Nix) to have a strong connection and create some splash plays down the field.

#2849. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#2848. Game Preview: Knicks vs Trail Blazers, November 21, 2026

As we all know, Madison Square Garden isn't just the most glorious gymnasium on the planet and revered in the world of sports; it's also famous for the long list of famous musicians who have performed under its unique bicycle-wheel ceiling. Now the Portland Trail Blazers are dropping by for a quick game of hoops on Saturday night, but they might actually be the one franchise that would be adept at bringing out the microphones as well. I mean, who wouldn't want to hear the newest rendition of all-timers like "Bust A Bucket" or "Rip City Rhapsody"?!

Not to disappoint anybody, but the days of Terry Porter and those shiny lycra dance outfits of the Blazers cheerleaders that would have made Cory Everson proud are long gone. Today's Rip City Rascals (coming in at 10-5) have their own way of entertaining a crowd, though, which is more than many would have expected before the season. With the return of Damian Lillard and a highly questionable move for "Pistol Petey" Ja Morant, the Blazers looked to be about five years late to the modern NBA party but atleast early it turns out they are anything but.

New head coach Micah Nori is making one of the more funky-looking rosters in the Association work so far, and he does it with a very modern, analytical approach. The Blazers shoot and make way more threes than their opponents (outscoring them by about 12+ points from beyond the arc), defend downtown better than 25 other teams in the NBA, and make sure Tony Brothers and his brothers-in-arms in the striped shirts have a living by drawing the second most fouls in the league.

Most of those foul numbers might be attributed to Deni Avdija. Only Luka from the Lakers and that feaky Greek in Miami stand at the charity stripe more frequently than the Israeli All-Star from Portland, leading to some fans questioning the ethics in his basketball bravado. But make no mistake about it, he is a serious talent, big-bodied and confident with a well-rounded game that can give every opponent problems. If he will be of any concern to the Knicks is not clear yet though; Avdija missed Portland's last two with a foot injury and figures to be a game-time decision.

But even without Avdija, Portland (road record 7-3) will present a very interesting matchup for your Knickerbockers. Damian Lillard seems to be rejuvenated after his Achilles injury, and a lot about his game feels a bit more wholesome in the jersey in which he always belonged. Behind him you have a Jrue Holiday coming off the bench, probably one of the more professional, classy guys you can find in the NBA these days. Cheekily, he also still packs quite a punch in his game, giving Nori first-class playmaking, tough D, and leadership in spades. If you think rattling these two old wily pros might get you anywhere, think again.

You could have more luck with some of the younger Blazers, but many of them bring certain elite skills to the table, too. Belgian baller Toumani Camara is one of the best defenders in the entire league, long, lanky, and extremely athletic. Seemingly plodding big man Donovan Clingan is getting more mobile by the minute and still hasn't lost his knack for gobbling up boards like a motivated Moses Malone did back in the day. Sidy Cissoko is another promising young wing, and former number two overall pick Scoot Henderson, while having a nightmarish season overall, can still break out for a big one as he did against Milwaukee the other night with 27 points.

What all those Blazers have in common is that they buy into Nori's system so far and operate with a clear-cut plan on the shiny hardwood. They extend their defense, making it hard to find anything downtown but also possible to do some damage in the midrange. On offense, they move the ball well, play five-out as much as they can (using Clingan as a high screen setter), and rely on balanced scoring night in and night out. It's a good formula, especially without Ja Morant, who has missed all but four games with a hamstring injury so far this year. The Knicks will have to touch the paint plenty to get that Portland defense moving, and they have to attack Lillard whenever possible on that end. Their superior depth could be a factor as well.

If the Blazers then throw a curveball and try to actually upend the Knicks' three-game winning streak by dragging them into a singing competition after all? No need to worry – we still got Tyler Kolek!

#2846. Oak Park falls at Millikan in CIF-SS Div. 7 football semifinals

Preston Azlein provided a brief glimmer of hope.

On the road against a formidable opponent, the Oak Park High football team, playing in its first CIF-Southern Section semifinal in 13 years, had hung around for a half.

Then Azlein tied the game 7-7 with a 30-yard interception return for a touchdown on the first play of the second half.

But host Long Beach Millikan answered with four consecutive touchdowns, including three in the fourth quarter, to grab the CIF-SS Division 7 semifinal, 35-14.

"They were so big up front," said Oak Park head coach Casey Webb. "They were massive. We just had no time to throw. It was hard to get the run game going. We just got beat up up front. We did the best we could."

"It's a tough one," said Webb. "But there's a lot to be proud of."

More: Pacifica's remarkable CIF-SS postseason run ends in Div. 2 semifinals

Oak Park was within 14-7 and driving in the fourth quarter, when a lost fumble on a run into the red zone proved decisive.

"That was the play that shifted the game," said Webb. "We were driving in to tie it. That deflated us."

Quarterback Bryson Kennedy hit receiver Gary Ferguson for a 37-yard scoring strike for the lone Oak Park offensive score in the fourth quarter.

Ryan Carter ran for three touchdowns for Millikan (6-7), which advances to play Paramount for the title this weekend.

"We knew they were big up front and it would be tough," Webb said, "but I didn't think it would be that tough."

Oak Park reached its first sectional semifinal since 2013 by beating Phelan-Serrano 41-29 and Redlands-Citrus Valley 30-27.

It battled the Moore League power within 7-0 at the half.

"We bent but we didn't break," Webb said. "We were playing field position back and forth."

#2845. Rockets sputter late in Denver 133-124

Tonight featured the Rockets third NBA Cup game, which was essentially a must-win after their opening NBA Cup round loss to Dallas. The Rockets would be playing in Denver, against a Nuggets team that seems to be finding its way after initially scuffling this season.

Many have mocked and derided the NBA Cup, and sure, it's a gimmick, and it's artificial, and so forth. But the sad truth is, professional sports are a gimmick, and are artificial, too. This is just a new wrinkle in the existing structure, not some sort of attack on the traditional and pure nature of NBA basketball. But even if you think it is some sort of assault, don't you want to watch players trying their hardest, during the regular season? Because they do try hard. These games actually are extra, because hyper competitive NBA players like being hyper competitive. It took a few years, but the players are there, they mean it (at least in the early games). By the time a 14 year old NBA fan today is in their mid 20s, the NBA Cup will always have been part of the NBA for them.

Anyhow, the Rockets won't be moving on in the NBA Cup this year. It's possible to move on with 3 wins, but not with maybe 2. Those who don't like this added competition are free to celebrate this.

As for tonight, there are two ways of looking at it.

The Rockets were on their fifth consecutive road game, which happened to be an NBA Cup game, at altitude in Denver. Denver is where good road trips go to die, and if they don't die there, it'll happen in Salt Lake City (you can look it up).

On top of the schedule, and the location, a bunch of player who had previously been kind of bad for Denver so far this season had excellent night. Aaron Gordon, Cam Johnson, and Christian Braun went 20-29 and threw in another 11 points from free throws, which they shot at 10-11.

The Rockets on the other hand, missed 12 free throws out of 35 attempts, shooting 66% to Denver's 93%.

The Rockets bench played 75 minutes and managed all of 12 points.

The Denver bench played 75 minutes as well, and contributed 37 points.

The game featured over 40 lead changes, and was anyone's game until the mid 4th quarter.

Maybe the altitude told on the Rockets starters, and Denver's stars, Jokic and Murray, whom the Rockets had held down most of the first half, came alive.

The duel between Sengun and Jokic was pretty much a dead heat individually.

Jokic went 10-15/2-3 3pt/7 rbs/11 ast/4 To for 26 points.

Sengun went 9-16/1-2 3pt/11 rbs/10 ast/2 TO/1 stl/1 blk for 28 points.

This one was painful, but not unexpected.

The second way is a little more alarming:

The Rockets held a narrow lead towards the end of a high scoring game. They couldn't keep scoring, and lost it.

As the game got tighter late, Denver clamped down on defense, and the Rockets simply couldn't execute offense.

The Rocket with the most shots was Fred VanVleet. This wasn't due to setting up Fred for shots, it was due to Fred often finding no passes, no one really doing much of anything, and being forced to take a late clock attempt.

FVV did this pretty well, but he also shot the most shots on the team, at 21, and was part of the Rockets two guards (including Amen) going 4-11 from the free throw line.

Kevin Durant took 9 shots, wasn't assertive and the Rockets couldn't get him the ball late, anyway.

Jabari Smith was on fire, but only got 8 attempts, of which he made 7.

The late game collapse, and it was a collapse in the final minutes, felt very familiar. Unable to get stops. Unable to execute on offense. There was no plan, no special action to get the Rockets best offensive players tonight, Sengun, and Jabari Smith, looks late. It was, again, a lot of Fred VanVleet dribbling under pressure, and often making a difficult midrange shot with no time remaining.

The level of competition was high, the level of intensity of that competition was high, so it's not unreasonable to have concerns about this outcome. Denver might be a first round playoff opponent, for example.

One game doesn't make a trend, and isn't proof of anything, but I saw in this game a painful reminder of painful games last season. That could just be bad memories, and sensitivity to certain sorts of issues. The season, and especially the playoffs, will tell us.

#2843. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.
