#2840. College football draft targets for the Cowboys: Week 12

We're looking ahead to see who may be on the Dallas Cowboys' draft card in 2027.

Georgia enters Williams-Brice Stadium as the nation's top-ranked team, but South Carolina has enough speed to make this a useful scouting assignment. The Bulldogs' front will test LaNorris Sellers and the Gamecocks' offensive line. On the other side, South Carolina has a corner and return specialist whose athletic ability should be apparent whenever the ball comes his way.

Gabe Harris Jr. has the type of frame NFL teams are always willing to develop. He has the length and mass to play as a traditional defensive end or stand-up outside linebacker. He is not a small speed rusher who has to win before an offensive tackle touches him. He can take contact and hold his ground.

His best work starts with his hands. Harris can lock out an offensive tackle and control the outside shoulder. Against the run, that lets him set a firm edge and forces the ball back toward Georgia's pursuit. He also has enough strength to squeeze a gap when a tight end tries to block him down. There is useful position flexibility here because Georgia can move him inside on passing downs and ask him to attack a guard.

Dallas needs young defensive ends who can survive on early downs. The roster needs another player who can set an edge without coming off the field whenever the opponent gets heavy. Harris would give the Cowboys a power complement in the rotation. He can align outside a tackle in an even front, play the strong side against a tight end and reduce inside in selected passing packages. That versatility would help Dallas keep its fronts less predictable.

Vicari Swain is one of the more entertaining cornerbacks in this class because his athleticism appears in two phases. He's listed at 6-foot and 193 pounds, and he brings genuine speed and excellent open-field instincts. Those traits help him in coverage, but they have also made him one of the country's most dangerous punt returners.

Swain is comfortable when he can play with vision on the quarterback. He closes quickly from off coverage and has the acceleration to recover when a receiver creates early separation. His movement skills also fit man coverage. He can mirror releases and carry vertical routes.

Swain fits the most important item on Dallas' shopping list. Joey Porter Jr. is the best Dallas has at corner and he's no guarantee for next season. Swain has the feet to compete outside and the quickness to receive work in the slot, giving the Cowboys options as the rest of the secondary changes around him.

His return value could help him dress immediately. A rookie corner selected outside the first round may begin as the fourth defensive back, but Swain can justify a game-day place through punts and coverage units while competing for defensive snaps. Dallas would gain an explosive special-teams player rather than waiting for a developmental corner to become useful.

Tennessee is trying to stay in the playoff race after grinding out a road win at Texas A&M. LSU has slipped out of the AP Top 25 at 6-4, but its roster still gives evaluators plenty to study. This matchup puts one of the SEC's quickest cornerbacks against Tennessee's vertical passing game and one of the nation's busiest running backs against LSU's defensive front.

P.J. Woodland is built differently from Swain. He's smaller at 5-foot-11 and 164 pounds, so there is no hiding the concern about his frame. What keeps him in the draft conversation is the quality of his coverage movement and his confidence playing the football.

Woodland is at his best in man coverage. His feet are quick at the line and he changes direction without wasted steps. He also has enough recovery speed to remain connected when a receiver threatens vertically. LSU trusts him on an island because he rarely panics when the ball is in the air. He tracks it and competes at the catch point despite giving away weight.

Dallas needs coverage talent, and Woodland's tape supplies it. His quick feet and man-cover ability could translate to the Cowboys' sub-packages, especially when the defense wants to pressure and cannot afford a corner losing immediately at the line.

DeSean Bishop has gone from a walk-on with one major college offer to the center of Tennessee's offense. The redshirt junior is a compact runner and his game is built around vision, balance and decisive cuts rather than rare size or track speed.

Bishop presses the line with patience. He allows blocks to form and gets vertical without extra movement. His low center of gravity helps him absorb glancing contact, while his footwork lets him redirect when the initial lane disappears. He is particularly effective on inside and split-zone concepts because he understands how to manipulate a linebacker before making his cut.

Javonte Williams can remain the lead back next season, but Dallas should keep adding inexpensive talent behind him. Bishop profiles as a complementary runner who can handle a series or take on a larger workload if the starter misses time.

His running style should fit the Cowboys' interior concepts. Bishop reads blocks cleanly and gets north quickly. That reliability would make him a sensible Day 3 selection, where Dallas can address the position without spending the premium capital needed at corner, tackle or edge.

#2839. Lakers needed a little bit from everyone in win against Kings

LOS ANGELES — If Friday's 106-104 win against the Sacramento Kings was any indicator, it's as if the Lakers have played bad teams so often over the past two weeks that they've started to become one.

"That was a—a win's a win," Lakers center Walker Kessler said as he labored into his postgame presser.

But just as the trillions of cells in the human body help fight a cold, Los Angeles got (and needed) a little bit of everything from everyone to get over this Sacramento flu.

Whether it was Kessler's first 3-point attempt and make of the season, seven assists from Quentin Grimes, who yet again hit a game-sealing three, five combined offensive rebounds from Jarred Vanderbilt and Sandro Mamukelashvili off the bench or 14 points from Matisse Thybulle, the Lakers needed every bit of it.

"Certain nights, whether it's the team, how we're playing, the atmosphere of the arena, you can just be a little flat," head coach JJ Redick said. "And I thought we just had to be tougher and pick up our energy. They accepted the challenge."

Friday marks the end of a stretch of four games in six nights for the Lakers, who improved to an NBA-best 14-3 record and are one of six teams to reach the 17-game mark. Los Angeles is also tied with three other teams for most back-to-back games thus far at four.

They haven't been absent of casualties during this early war of attrition. Austin Reaves missed his fourth consecutive game with a left heel bruise he suffered against Milwaukee. That the Lakers have certainly felt the impact of playing without their second-best player is no revelation, but Luka Dončić has perhaps felt his absence the most.

Dončić, the league's leading scorer, extended his shooting slump on Friday, scoring 25 points on 2-of-9 shooting from three in another game where he was the focal point of the opposing defense every second he was on the floor. Dating back to the Milwaukee game, his shooting percentages have dipped to 41.4% from the field and 30.6% from beyond the arc.

The double-teams and blitzes he saw earlier in the season were less effective when Reaves was there to make the defense pay. But without him, the weight of the offense is much heavier on Dončić.

That's where Los Angeles's role players come in. Just as inconsistent as their roles have been so far, their impact on winning has been just as consistent. Redick said postgame that their plan in the offseason was to surround Dončić and Reaves with complementary players, and that despite having played three-quarters of a game with both of them available over their last seven games, the Lakers are 6-1.

"It's a credit to the group [for] sticking with it," Redick added. "Basketball's a game of mistakes. We played through some mistakes tonight, just like we did the other night against Charlotte. Good win. We'll take it."

The locker room is beset by these shooting slumps. Dončić, Mamu, Ziaire Williams and Jake LaRavia, among others, are simply going through one of those stretches. Redick plans to use the Lakers' forthcoming two-day break — a rarity so far this season — to ensure the team continues to stay sharp and get shots up.

After Monday's rematch against Utah, the team will enjoy another three days off before clashing with San Antonio. Just as Grimes broke through a rough start to the season, Redick believes others will come around, no matter how much a slump can weigh on someone.

To that point, as Redick recently stayed up late on the iMovie app on his phone, cooking up a two-minute highlight video of six-time major champion golfer Rory McIlroy to show his players — a video he humbly thought was "well-produced and edited" — one of McIlroy's quotes stuck with him during this trying time:

"When you're not playing well, it feels like your good game is so far away. And then once you start playing well, you realize it was never that far away to begin with."

#2838. D.J. Reed, Sione Vaki listed as questionable for the Lions

Lions cornerback D.J. Reed has missed the last two games with a hip injury, but his return to the lineup remains on the table heading into the weekend.

Reed is listed as questionable to face the Buccaneers after three limited practices this week. Reed said earlier this week that he felt he came back too fast from a hamstring injury last season and does not want to make the same mistake this time around.

Running back Sione Vaki (ankle) also drew a questionable tag. He had six carries for 28 yards and one catch for 17 yards in last Sunday’s win over the Patriots.

Center Cade Mays (ankle) has been ruled out. Linebacker Joe Bachie (knee), cornerback Khalil Dorsey (ankle), defensive lineman Tyler Lacy (shoulder), and cornerback Ennis Rakestraw (shoulder) round out the questionable group for Detroit.

#2837. Broncos announce uniform for Sunday's game vs. Raiders

The Denver Broncos have announced their uniform for Sunday's game against the Las Vegas Raiders.

The Broncos will wear orange jerseys with white pants and white socks against their AFC West rival in Week 11. Denver is undefeated (2-0) in orange jerseys so far this season, and after the Raiders game, the Broncos will only have one more primary orange jersey game on their 2026 regular-season schedule.

Denver announced its full uniform schedule for the 2026 campaign in August. The Broncos will wear white jerseys nine times, orange jerseys four times, alternate navy twice and throwback blue twice this fall.

Here's a preview of the team's look for Sunday's game against the Raiders:

Earlier this season, the Broncos wore their fan-favorite throwback uniform on Thursday Night Football against the Seattle Seahawks in Week 6. They'll wear the throwbacks again against the Los Angeles Chargers in Week 18.

The Broncos wore their alternate navy jersey against the Los Angeles Rams on Sunday Night Football in Week 3, and they will wear it again against the Buffalo Bills on Christmas Day in Week 16. The club also has a new alternate uniform set to arrive in 2027.

Denver will announce pants and socks combinations the week of each game this season.

#2835. Antonio Brown critical of Steelers WR DK Metcalf amidst struggles

It's no secret at this point that DK Metcalf has been a massive disappointment ever since he was traded to the Pittsburgh Steelers. His second season in Pittsburgh has been just as disastrous as the first, as he is second in the NFL in drops with 6, while leading all receivers in penalties.

Metcalf leads the Steelers in yards with 432, but has hauled in just 35 of his 71 targets through nine games this season. The receiver admitted to the media that this was the first time he's dealt with these kinds of struggles in his career, but a former Steeler had advice for Metcalf.

Appearing on "The Matchup" podcast with former Steelers teammate Emmanuel Sanders and Chris Harris Jr., former Steelers standout wide receiver Antonio Brown told Metcalf he simply needs to come back to the ball more.

"I see DK Metcalf trying to box a guy out," Brown said. "Yo bro, you gotta go back to the ball. Take your nose to the ball. I feel like guys gotta be more confident taking their nose to the ball. You gotta take your nose to the ball first, and your eyes gotta catch it. It's eyes, nose, hands. Most of these guys, they just use hands, no eyes. You gotta have the concentration. You gotta be focused to be a good pass catcher."

This sounds like shockingly decent advice coming from Brown, who has gained a reputation for being an internet troll at best over the course of the last few seasons. The concentration quote makes a lot of sense given the sheer number of drops Metcalf has had this season.

Simply put, something has to give with Metcalf. Perhaps with the return of Michael Pittman Jr. and Roman Wilson to the Steelers' offense, things will look smoother for Metcalf and the offense as a whole. Expect Aaron Rodgers to continue to rely on Metcalf down the stretch as the Steelers sit firmly in the playoff picture.
